Gaussian Multiplicative Chaos for Gaussian Orthogonal and Symplectic Ensembles
Pax Kivimae

Abstract
We study the characteristic polynomials of both the Gaussian Orthogonal and Symplectic Ensembles. We show that for both ensembles, powers of the absolute value of the characteristic polynomials converge in law to Gaussian multiplicative chaos measures after normalization for sufficiently small real powers. The main tool is a new asymptotic relation between the fractional moments of the absolute characteristic polynomials of Gaussian Orthogonal, Unitary, and Symplectic Ensembles.



1
Introduction

The Gaussian Orthogonal/Unitary/Symplectic Ensembles are probability measures on the space of
N\times N
Symmetric/Hermitian/Quaternionic-Hermitian matrices with density

(1.1)

\lx@paragraphsign_{\beta}(A)=\frac{1}{Z_{N,\beta}}\exp(-N\beta\text{\rm Tr}(A^{2})),

where here
\beta=1/2/4
, respectively, and
Z_{N,\beta}
is a normalization constant. We will write
A_{N,\beta}
for a matrix sampled from the measure (
1.1
).

The focus of this paper will be the study of the (absolute) characteristic polynomial of matrices sampled from these ensembles in the large
N
limit. Our understanding of the behavior of such random characteristic polynomials for these (and other) matrix ensembles has seen much progress in recent years (see, for example
[
4
]
and the references therein). Much of this progress has been motivated by conjectures on these processes due to Fyodorov, Hiary, Keating, and Simm in
[
23
,
20
,
21
]
, who predict a detailed picture of the extremal values of these processes by relating these characteristic polynomials to objects from the theory of log-correlated fields. Key to this picture is the foundational work of Fyodorov, Khoruzhenko, and Simm
[
22
]
, which shows that for
\beta=2
, the normalized logarithm of the characteristic polynomial converges (in a suitable sense) to a log-correlated Gaussian random field, expanding the picture derived by Hughes, Keating, and O’Connell
[
24
]
in the Unitary case. More explicitly, in
[
22
]
they construct a centered Gaussian field
X
on
(-1,1)
with covariance kernel

(1.2)

\mathbb{E}X(x)X(y)=-\frac{1}{2}\log|2(x-y)|.

Due to the divergence of this covariance kernel on the diagonal,
X
does not exist as a random function, but instead, they show that it may be constructed as a random variable valued in a suitable space of distributions. They then proceed to show that the normalized logarithm of
|\det(A_{N,2}-xI)|
converges in law to
X
with respect to the topology given by a suitable Sobolev norm.

A key tool used to understand the geometric properties of log–correlated fields (such as
X
) is their associated (GMC) Gaussian multiplicative chaos measures, introduced by Kahane
[
27
]
, which are roughly given by regularizing the exponential of the field. For more background on log-correlated fields and GMC measures, the reader is invited to consult
[
37
]
.

In view of the results of
[
22
,
24
]
, one thus expects that the asymptotic behavior of the characteristic polynomials of these ensembles and their powers should be described by a family of GMC measures, and indeed it is from this perspective that
[
23
,
21
]
were able to formulate their conjectures. Much progress has been achieved in establishing this picture rigorously. In particular, for
\beta=2
, Berestycki, Webb, and Wong
[
4
]
establish that small enough powers of the absolute value of the characteristic polynomial can be described in the large
N
limit by a Gaussian multiplicative chaos (GMC) measure. Explicitly, they construct a family of random measures
\mu_{\alpha}
, for
\alpha\in(0,2)
, which may be heuristically written as

(1.3)

\mu_{\alpha}(dx)=\exp\left(\alpha X(x)-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[X(x)^{2}]\right)dx.

They then show that when viewed as random measures on
(-1,1)
, the sequence

(1.4)

\frac{|\det(A_{N,2}-xI)|^{\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}[|\det(A_{N,2}-xI)|^{\alpha}]}dx

converges in law to
\mu_{\alpha}
with respect to the topology of weak convergence of measures. Our main result will be an analog of this result for the Gaussian Orthogonal and Symplectic ensembles (i.e.,
\beta=1,4
).

Theorem 1.1
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1/\sqrt{2})
, we have as
N\to\infty
that

(1.5)

\frac{|\det(A_{2N,1}-xI)|^{\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}[|\det(A_{2N,1}-xI)|^{\alpha}]}dx\Rightarrow\mu_{\sqrt{2}\alpha}(dx)

in law with respect to the topology of weak convergence of measures.

Similarly, for
\alpha\in(0,\sqrt{2})
we have as
N\to\infty
that

(1.6)

\frac{|\det(A_{N,4}-xI)|^{\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}[|\det(A_{N,4}-xI)|^{\alpha}]}dx\Rightarrow\mu_{\alpha/\sqrt{2}}(dx)

in law with respect to the topology of weak convergence of measures.

We note that some analogous results are known outside of the case of Gaussian ensembles. In particular, Webb
[
41
]
showed that suitably small powers (more precisely powers in the
L^{2}
-regime) of the characteristic polynomial of a Haar distributed random unitary matrix converges in law to a certain GMC measure on the unit circle, after a suitable normalization. This result was later extended by Nikula, Saksman, and Webb
[
35
]
to the larger
L^{1}
-regime, which is believed to be optimal. Additionally, Forkel and Keating
[
17
]
have shown a similar result for the characteristic polynomials of matrices sampled from the Haar measures on both the orthogonal and symplectic groups.

In all of these cases though, the results rely on precise asymptotics for fractional moments of characteristic polynomials (for example, see Sections 3 and 4 of
[
4
]
). Depending on the case, this becomes equivalent to obtaining asymptotics for certain Toeplitz, Hankel, or Toeplitz+Hankel determinants associated with certain measures with Fischer-Hartwig singularities (see
[
19
]
for further background). These asymptotics have been computed in many cases through the use of Riemann-Hilbert methods (see, for example,
[
30
]
). These methods though, are specific to the case of
\beta=2
, with no clear generalization to the
\beta=1,4
cases.

Here we take a different approach to prove Theorem
1.1
. Instead of calculating the fractional moments directly (which appear to still be inaccessible to current methods), we instead relate fractional moments of
|\det(A_{2N,1}-xI)|
and
|\det(A_{N,4}-xI)|
to those of
|\det(A_{2N,2}-xI)|
. For notational convenience in stating our result, here and elsewhere, we will denote

(1.7)

D_{N,\beta}(x)=\det(A_{N,\beta}-xI).

Our main tool in proving Theorem
1.1
is then the following asymptotic relation, which may also be of independent interest.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
m\geq 1
, and for each
1\leq i\leq m
, let us take
\alpha_{i}>0
and
\lambda_{i}\in(-1,1)
such that
-1<\lambda_{m}<\cdots<\lambda_{1}<1
. Let
\mathcal{W}
be a smooth, compactly-supported function that coincides with a polynomial on a neighborhood of
[-1,1]
. Then we have that

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigg[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}(A_{2N,1}))}

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{2N,1}(\lambda_{i})|^{\alpha_{i}}\ \Bigg]\times\mathbb{E}\Bigg[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(2\mathcal{W}(A_{N,4}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{N,4}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha_{i}}\Bigg]

(1.9)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(2\mathcal{W}(A_{2N,2}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{2N,2}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha_{i}}\Bigg](1+O(N^{-1/6})).

Moreover the error term is uniform over compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
.

Remark 1.3
.

Theorem
1.2
may be compared to the following relation given by Baik and Rains
[
3
]
(also noted in
[
19
]
). Denote by
O_{N},U_{N}
, and
S_{N}
, a random matrix sampled from the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SO(N)}
,
\mathrm{U(N)}
, and
\mathrm{Sp(N)}
, respectively. Then for a large class of functions
g
(including integrable functions), we have the following relation

(1.10)

\mathbb{E}[\det(g(O_{2N+2}))]\mathbb{E}[\det(g(S_{2N}))]=\mathbb{E}[|\det(g(U_{2N}))|^{2}].

For other occurrences of GMC measures in random matrix theory, see
[
32
,
8
]
. We also mention the work of Lambert, Ostrovsky, and Simm
[
33
]
, where they establish the convergence of a regularized version of
\log(|D_{N,\beta}(x)|)
to a log-correlated field for arbitrary
\beta>0
. Additionally, we also mention the recent work of Bourgade, Mody, and Pain
[
6
]
, where they establish normalized fluctuations of
\log(|D_{N,\beta}(x)|)
at a finite collection of points. In the work of Claeys, Fahs, Lambert, and Webb
[
10
]
, a similar convergence to a GMC measure is shown for the exponential of the eigenvalue counting function, from which they derive optimal bounds on eigenvalue fluctuations. Lastly, for some beautiful relationships between log-correlated fields, random matrices, and the behavior of special functions in number theory, one may consult
[
29
,
21
,
23
]
, and the references therein.

Lastly, we also remark that the restriction to the even case for
\beta=1
in Theorem
1.1
appears to be a technical artifact of our method of proof, which relies crucially on Theorem
1.2
. Similarly, we note that the parameter ranges for
\alpha
in Theorem
1.1
amount to only a strict subset of the
L^{2}
-phase, which occurs for
\alpha<\sqrt{\beta}
. This restriction as well is a limitation of our method and is due to having to rely on the convergence of the
\beta=2
at larger powers, as is explained below.

1.1
Construction of GMC and sketch of the proof of Theorem
1.1
from Theorem
1.2

We now outline our method to obtain Theorem
1.1
from Theorem
1.2
. Details are given in Section
3
. We begin by recalling the basics of the construction of the GMC measure (
1.3
). We first recall a sequence of Gaussian processes on
(-1,1)
, denoted
X_{M}
, such that a.s.
X_{M}\Rightarrow X
as
M\to\infty
in the distributional sense. This decomposition comes from the identity (see, for example, Appendix C of
[
36
]
) that for
x,y\in(-1,1)
, we have that

(1.11)

-\frac{1}{2}\log(2|x-y|)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}T_{k}(x)T_{k}(y),

where
T_{k}
is
k
-th Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind defined by the relation
T_{k}(\cos(\theta))=\cos(k\theta)
for
\theta\in(0,2\pi]
. The polynomial
T_{k}
is also the
k
-th orthogonal polynomial with respect to the measure
\mu_{as}
, where
\mu_{as}(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}(1-x^{2})^{-1/2}I(|x|<1)
denotes the (shifted) arcsine distribution. In particular, from this, we have the following (formal) distributional identity

(1.12)

X(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{Z_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}T_{k}(x),

where
(Z_{k})_{k}
is a sequence of i.i.d standard Gaussians random variables. We define

(1.13)

X_{M}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\frac{Z_{k}}{\sqrt{k}}T_{k}(x),

which is the formal projection of
X
onto polynomials of degree-
M
in
L^{2}(\mu_{as})
.

If we define
\mu_{M,\alpha}(dx)=\exp(\alpha X_{M}(x)-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[X_{M}(x)^{2}])dx
, it is shown in
[
4
]
that for
\alpha\in(0,2)
, the measures
\mu_{M,\alpha}
converge weakly in law to a nontrivial limiting measure, which will be our
\mu_{\alpha}
. In addition, for
\alpha\in(0,\sqrt{2})
, it is shown that
\mu_{M,\alpha}
converges to
\mu_{\alpha}
in the
L^{2}
-norm as well.

We now define

(1.14)

\mu_{N,\beta,\alpha}(dx)=\frac{|D_{N,\beta}(x)|^{\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}[|D_{N,\beta}(x)|^{\alpha}]}dx.

In addition, we introduce processes
X_{N,M,\beta}
, similar to
X_{M}
, but relating instead to
\log(|D_{N,\beta}(x)|)
. As the eigenvalues of
A_{N,\beta}
may live outside of
(-1,1)
, we first must smoothly extend
T_{k}
to a compactly-supported function. In particular, let us fix some
\epsilon>0
and choose, for each
k
, a function
\tilde{T}_{k}(x)
which is smooth and of compact support, and such that
\tilde{T}_{k}(x)=T_{k}(x)
on
(-1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)
. We define

(1.15)

X_{N,M,\beta}(x)=-\sum_{k=1}^{M}\frac{2}{k}\text{\rm Tr}(\tilde{T}_{k}(A_{N,\beta}))T_{k}(x),\;\;\;\;\mu_{N,M,\beta,\alpha}(dx)=\frac{e^{\alpha X_{N,M,\beta}(x)}}{\mathbb{E}e^{\alpha X_{N,M,\beta}(x)}}dx.

For a measure
\mu
on
(-1,1)
, and a function
f
, let us denote
\mu(f):=\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\mu(dx)
. We note that by a standard argument (see Section 4 of
[
28
]
), there is no loss of generality to replace the weak convergence in Theorem
1.1
with vague convergence. That is, it suffices to show for any fixed continuous compactly-supported function
\varphi:(-1,1)\to[0,\infty)
, and for
\alpha<\sqrt{\beta/2}
, that we have that

(1.16)

\mu_{N,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi)\Rightarrow\mu_{\alpha\sqrt{2/\beta}}(\varphi)

in law as
N\to\infty
for
\beta=1,4
(where
N
only runs over even numbers if
\beta=1
).

For
\beta=1,2,4
and
\alpha>0
and
M
fixed we have that

(1.17)

\mu_{N,M,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi)\Rightarrow\mu_{M,\alpha\sqrt{2/\beta}}(\varphi)

in law as
N\to\infty
. This result is a consequence of the classical asymptotics for linear statistics given in
[
26
]
. In particular, this follows immediately by repeating the proof of the
\beta=2
case given in Section 6 of
[
4
]
, as the results of
[
26
]
hold for all
\beta=1,2,4
. Noting the inequality,

(1.18)

\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}(\mu_{N,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi)-\mu_{\alpha\sqrt{2/\beta}}(\varphi))^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}(\mu_{N,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi)-\mu_{N,M,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}+

(1.19)

\mathbb{E}(\mu_{N,M,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi)-\mu_{M,\alpha\sqrt{2/\beta}}(\varphi))^{2}+\mathbb{E}(\mu_{M,\alpha\sqrt{2/\beta}}(\varphi)-\mu_{\alpha\sqrt{2/\beta}}(\varphi))^{2},

we see that when taking the limit
N\to\infty
and then the limit
M\to\infty
, the second and third terms on the right-hand side vanish, so to demonstrate (
1.16
) it suffices to show that for
\beta=1,4
, we have that

(1.20)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\mu_{N,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi)-\mu_{N,M,\beta,\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}=0,

where again
N
only ranges over even numbers if
\beta=1
.

One should note that in the case of
\beta=2
, the left-hand side of (
1.20
) is shown to vanish in
[
4
]
. The key step will then be to define an additional pair of measures

(1.21)

\nu_{N,\alpha}(dx)=\frac{|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{\alpha}|D_{N,4}(x)|^{2\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}\big[|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{\alpha}\big]\mathbb{E}\big[|D_{N,4}(x)|^{2\alpha}\big]}dx,\;\;\;\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(dx)=\frac{e^{\alpha X_{2N,M,1}(x)}e^{2\alpha X_{N,M,4}(x)}}{\mathbb{E}e^{\alpha X_{2N,M,1}(x)}\mathbb{E}e^{2\alpha X_{N,M,4}(x)}}dx,

where
A_{2N,1}
and
A_{N,4}
are sampled independently. We observe that
\mathbb{E}_{A_{2N,1}}[\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)]=\mu_{N,4,2\alpha}(\varphi)
an
\mathbb{E}_{A_{N,4}}[\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)]=\mu_{2N,1,\alpha}(\varphi)
, where
\mathbb{E}_{A_{2N,1}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{A_{N,4}}
denote expectation with respect to
A_{2N,1}
and
A_{N,4}
, respectively. A similar result holds for
\nu_{N,M,\alpha}
. A key observation is that Jensen’s inequality implies that

(1.22)

\mathbb{E}(\mu_{N,4,\alpha}(\varphi)-\mu_{N,M,4,\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}_{A_{2N,1}}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)-\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi)))^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)-\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}.

With a similar observation at
\beta=1
, this implies that to show (
1.20
), it suffices to show that for
\alpha<1/\sqrt{2}

(1.23)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)-\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}=0.

In addition, it will suffice to show that

(1.24)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)-\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}=\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\mu_{2N,2,2\alpha}(\varphi)-\mu_{2N,M,2,2\alpha}(\varphi))^{2},

as the latter is shown to vanish in Proposition 2.9 of
[
4
]
. Expanding both sides of (
1.24
), we will see that all the integrands coincide pointwise as
N\to\infty
by Theorem
1.2
, reducing the remaining work to showing that the error term is suitably uniform. Details of this are given in Section
3
.

1.2
Outline of the proof of Theorem
1.2

We now outline the method of proof of Theorem
1.2
. Let us choose some
m\geq 1
and
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
1.2
. We will denote the polynomial coinciding with
\mathcal{W}
on
(-1,1)
as
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, and denote the difference as
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W}_{0}
. We will omit the data
(N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
from the notation when such a choice is clear from the context. We will reduce the proof of Theorem
1.2
to the computation of the asymptotics of the determinant of an
N
-by-
N
matrix
\Delta_{N}:=\Delta_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
, defined in (
2.30
). More specifically, we show that

(1.25)

\frac{\mathbb{E}[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}(A_{2N,1}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{2N,1}(\lambda_{i})|^{\alpha_{i}}]\mathbb{E}[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(2\mathcal{W}(A_{N,4}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{N,4}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha_{i}}]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(2\mathcal{W}(A_{2N,2}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{2N,2}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha_{i}}]}=\sqrt{\det(\Delta_{N})}.

This reduces the proof of Theorem
1.2
to the computation of
\det(\Delta_{N})
.

This representation was essentially observed as Remark 2.4 of
[
38
]
and will essentially follow (up to normalization constants) from the representations presented in
[
2
]
. This matrix
\Delta_{N}
originally appeared in
[
42
]
, and was used to express the correlation functions of Orthogonal and Symplectic ensembles, and is shown there to be quite sparse. In particular in the case
\mathcal{W}=0
, we have that
\Delta_{N}
coincides with the identity outside of an
(m+1)
-by-
(m+1)
block. In the case of
\mathcal{W}\neq 0
we will show that
\Delta_{N}
is the identity outside a
(m+\max(\deg(\mathcal{W}_{0})-1,1))
-by-
(m+\max(\deg(\mathcal{W}_{0})-1,1))
block, up to an error term which we show is exponentially small in Section
5
.

In either case, the entries of this distinguished block may be expressed explicitly in terms of the orthogonal polynomials with respect to the measure

(1.26)

w_{N}(x):=w(x;N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=e^{-2Nx^{2}}e^{2\mathcal{W}(x)}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|x-\lambda_{i}|^{2\alpha_{i}}.

The asymptotics of the orthogonal polynomials with respect to this measure is known in the case of
\mathcal{W}=0
by
[
30
]
, and
\mathcal{W}\neq 0
by
[
4
]
, and are recalled in detail in Section
4
. With this information, we are able to compute the asymptotics of this block matrix to sufficient order to compute the first-order behavior of
\det(\Delta_{N})
. This is done in Section
6
.

We remark that computations of
\Delta_{N}
for other measures form a key step in the proof of universality for the correlations functions of various symmetric and symplectic ensembles
[
13
,
14
,
12
,
38
]
.

1.3
Structure of the paper

In Section
2
, we prove (
1.25
) and reduce the asymptotic computation of
\det(\Delta_{N})
to Propositions
2.6
and
2.9
. In addition, we provide a modification of Theorem
1.2
, Proposition
2.10
, which applies in the case of (subcritical) merging singularities and reduces its proof to Proposition
2.13
. In Section
3
we complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
which by the argument given above is reduced to demonstrating (
1.24
). The bulk of this statement will follow from Theorem
1.2
and Proposition
2.10
. In Section
4
, we provide asymptotics for the orthogonal polynomials with respect to
w_{N}
, while in Section
5
we employ these asymptotics to prove Proposition
2.6
, which effectively allows us to neglect all but finitely many entries of
\det(\Delta_{N})
. In Section
6
, we prove Proposition
2.9
, which consists of computing the highest order behavior of certain integrals of orthogonal polynomials, by employing classical methods of steepest descent to the asymptotics of Section
4
. Furthermore, in Section
7
, we will provide a proof of Proposition
2.13
. The methods of this section will be similar to those of Section
6
, and indeed the cases of Proposition
2.9
and Proposition
2.13
have significant overlap.

In addition, the author would like to thank an anonymous reviewer for finding numerous typos and errors earlier drafts.

2
Preliminary Results

In this section, we will prove (
1.25
). In addition, we will analyze the terms of
\Delta_{N}
to reduce the computation of
\det(\Delta_{N})
to a sequence of integrals and quantities given in terms of the orthogonal polynomials of
w_{N}
.

We will assume henceforth that
N>m+\deg(\mathcal{W}_{0})
. We define the unnormalized expected characteristic polynomial as

(2.1)

\Phi_{N,\beta}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=\frac{1}{N!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}|\Delta_{N}(x)|^{\beta}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(e^{-N\beta x_{i}^{2}+\mathcal{W}(x_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{m}|x_{i}-\lambda_{j}|^{\alpha_{j}})dx^{N},

where here
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i}=(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})
,
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots\alpha_{m})
, and

\Delta_{N}(x)=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{i}-x_{j})

denotes the Vandermonde determinant. We note that (see Chapter 1 of
[
18
]
)

(2.2)

\Phi_{N,\beta}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=Z_{N,\beta}\mathbb{E}e^{\text{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}(A_{N,\beta}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|\det(A_{N,\beta}-\lambda_{i}I)|^{\alpha_{i}}.

We will need the following relation.

Lemma 2.1
.

For all
N\geq 1
, we have that
Z_{2N,1}Z_{N,4}=2^{2N}Z_{2N,2}
.

Proof.

The values of
Z_{N,\beta}
are known exactly as

(2.3)

Z_{N,\beta}=(2N)^{-\beta\frac{N(N-1)}{4}-N/2}(N!)^{-1}\beta^{-\frac{N}{2}-\beta\frac{N(N-1)}{4}}(2\pi)^{N/2}\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(1+j\beta/2)}{\Gamma(1+\beta/2)}.

This
follows from equation 1.163 of
[
18
]
, up to rescaling. In view of this, we may rewrite
Z_{2N,1}Z_{N,4}/Z_{2N,2}
as

(2.4)

\frac{(4N)^{\frac{-2N(2N-1)}{4}-N}(2N)^{-N(N-1)-N/2}}{(4N)^{-\frac{2N(2N-1)}{2}-N}}\frac{4^{-N/2-N(N-1)}\Gamma(2)^{2N}}{2^{-N-N(2N-1)}\Gamma(3)^{N}}\frac{(2\pi)^{N/2}}{\Gamma(3/2)^{2N}N!}\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{2N}\Gamma(1+j/2)\prod_{j=1}^{N}\Gamma(1+2j)}{\prod_{j=1}^{2N}\Gamma(1+j)}.

We observe that

(2.5)

\frac{(4N)^{\frac{-2N(2N-1)}{4}-N}(2N)^{-N(N-1)-N/2}}{(4N)^{-\frac{2N(2N-1)}{2}-N}}=2^{N^{2}-\frac{N}{2}},\quad\frac{4^{-N/2-N(N-1)}\Gamma(2)^{2N}}{2^{-N-N(2N-1)}\Gamma(3)^{N}}=1.

We may also obtain that

(2.6)

\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{2N}\Gamma(1+j/2)\prod_{j=1}^{N}\Gamma(1+2j)}{\prod_{j=1}^{2N}\Gamma(1+j)}=\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(j+1/2)\Gamma(1+j)}{\Gamma(2j)}=

(2.7)

N!\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma(j+1/2)\Gamma(j)}{\Gamma(2j)}=N!\pi^{N/2}\prod_{j=1}^{N}2^{1-2j}=N!\pi^{N/2}2^{-N^{2}},

where in the second to last equality we have employed the Legendre duplication formula (see 6.1.18 of
[
1
]
)

(2.8)

\Gamma(z)\Gamma(z+1/2)/\Gamma(2z)=2^{1-2z}\pi.

In particular we may rewrite (
2.4
) as

(2.9)

2^{-N/2}\pi^{N/2}\frac{(2\pi)^{N/2}}{\Gamma(3/2)^{2N}}=\frac{\pi^{N}}{\Gamma(3/2)^{2N}}=2^{2N},

where in the last step we used that
\Gamma(3/2)=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\pi}
. As (
2.4
) coincides with
Z_{2N,1}Z_{N,4}/Z_{2N,2}
we obtain the desired result.
∎

From this, we see that

(2.10)

\frac{\mathbb{E}[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(\mathcal{W}(A_{2N,1}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{2N,1}(\lambda_{i})|^{\alpha_{i}}]\mathbb{E}[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(2\mathcal{W}(A_{N,4}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{N,4}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha_{i}}]}{\mathbb{E}[e^{\text{\rm Tr}(2\mathcal{W}(A_{2N,2}))}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|D_{2N,2}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha_{i}}]}=

(2.11)

2^{2N}\frac{\Phi_{2N,1}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})\Phi_{N,4}(2\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{2\alpha_{i}\}_{i})}{\Phi_{2N,2}(2\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{2\alpha_{i}\}_{i})}.

We now recall a classical representation of these integrals. For simplicity, for the remainder of the section, we will fix
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
and denote, as above,

(2.12)

w_{N}(x)=w(x;N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}).

We define a family of
N
-by-
N
matrices defined for
1\leq i,j\leq N
as

(2.13)

[M_{N}^{2}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})]_{ij}=\int x^{i+j-2}w_{N}(x)dx,

(2.14)

[M^{4}_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})]_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\int((x^{i-1})^{\prime}(x^{j-1})-(x^{i-1})(x^{j-1})^{\prime})w_{N}(x)dx,

(2.15)

[M^{1}_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})]_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\int\int x^{j-1}y^{i-1}\;\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\sqrt{w_{N}(x)w_{N}(y)}dxdy.

We note that the latter two matrices are antisymmetric, while the first is symmetric. The relationship between these matrices and the various
\Phi_{N,\beta}
is classical.

Lemma 2.2
.

We have for all
N\geq 1
, that

(2.16)

\det(M^{2}_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))=\Phi_{2,N}(2\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{2\alpha_{i}\}_{i}),

(2.17)

\mathrm{pf}(M^{1}_{2N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))=2^{-N}\Phi_{1,2N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}),

(2.18)

\mathrm{pf}(M^{4}_{2N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))=2^{-N}\Phi_{4,N}(2\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{2\alpha_{i}\}_{i}),

where here
\mathrm{pf}
denotes the Pfaffian.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Propositions 5.2.1, 6.1.8, and 6.3.4 of
[
18
]
.
∎

From this we may further write (
2.11
) as

(2.19)

\frac{\mathrm{pf}(M^{1}_{2N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))\mathrm{pf}(M^{4}_{2N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))}{\det(M^{2}_{2N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))}.

To relate the various
M^{\beta}_{N}
further we introduce operators
J_{N}^{\pm 1}:=J^{\pm 1}(N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
by

(2.20)

J_{N}=w_{N}(x)^{-1/2}\frac{d}{dx}w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=\frac{d}{dx}-2Nx+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\alpha_{i}}{x-\lambda_{i}}+\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(x),

(2.21)

J_{N}^{-1}f(x)=w_{N}(x)^{-1/2}\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)f(y)w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dy.

We note that as long as
f
is continuously differentiable and of sub-exponential growth, then

(2.22)

(J_{N}J_{N}^{-1}f)(x)=w_{N}(x)^{-1/2}\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)f(y)w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dy=f(x),

and similarly
(J_{N}J_{N}^{-1}f)(x)=f(x)
. As this will always be the case in this work, we will use these relations without mention. The utility of these operators is clear from the following classical relations.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
f
and
g
be polynomials. Then

(2.23)

\int(J_{N}^{-1}f)(x)g(x)w_{N}(x)dx=\frac{1}{2}\int\int g(x)f(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\sqrt{w_{N}(x)w_{N}(y)}dxdy,

(2.24)

\int(J_{N}f)(x)g(x)w_{N}(x)dx=\frac{1}{2}\int(f^{\prime}(x)g(x)-f(x)g^{\prime}(x))w_{N}(x)dx.

We note that in view of this lemma, we may express, for even
N
,

(2.25)

[M_{N}^{1}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})]_{ij}=\int(J_{N}^{-1}x^{i-1})x^{j-1}w_{N}(x)dx,

(2.26)

[M_{N}^{4}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})]_{ij}=\int(J_{N}x^{i-1})x^{j-1}w_{N}(x)dx.

To further simplify this, it will be useful to recall the sequence of orthogonal polynomials related to
w_{N}
. Namely, let
p_{i}(x):=p_{i}(x;N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
denote the unique sequence of polynomials, with
p_{i}
of degree-
i
, such that

(2.27)

\int p_{i}(x)p_{j}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=\delta_{ij},

and such that if we denote the leading coefficient of
p_{i}
as
\kappa_{i}:=\kappa_{i}(N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
, we have that
\kappa_{i}>0
. It is important to note that each
p_{i}
is
N
-dependant. As we will keep
N
fixed throughout the remainder of this section, we will omit
N
, as well
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
, from the notation for the
p_{i}
below.

We introduce the Christoffel-Darboux kernel

(2.28)

K_{N}(x,y)=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}p_{i}(x)p_{i}(y),

as well as the corresponding operator

(2.29)

(\Pi_{N}f)(x)=\int K_{N}(x,y)f(y)w_{N}(y)dy.

Let us denote the space of polynomials of degree less than
m
as
\mathcal{P}_{m}
. We will denote the space of polynomials as
\mathcal{P}
. Both of these inherit the inner-product
(f,g)_{w,N}=\int f(x)g(x)w_{N}(x)dx
. With respect to this inner-product
\Pi_{N}
is the orthogonal projection of
\mathcal{P}
onto
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. We will also use the notation
\Pi_{N}^{\perp}=I-\Pi_{N}
. We introduce an
N\times N
matrix given for
1\leq i,j\leq N
as

(2.30)

[\Delta_{N}]_{ij}=\int(J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}J_{N}p_{i-1})(x)p_{j-1}(x)w_{N}(x)dx.

We observe that the matrix
\Delta_{N}
is simply the representation of the operator
\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}J_{N}\Pi_{N}
with respect basis of
\mathcal{P}_{N}
given by the orthogonal polynomials. We will often refer to
\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}J_{N}\Pi_{N}
as
\Delta_{N}
when no confusion may arise. We have the following formula.

Lemma 2.4
.

For
N
even, we have that

(2.31)

\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{pf}(M^{1}_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))\mathrm{pf}(M^{4}_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))}{\det(M^{2}_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}))}\bigg]^{2}=\det(\Delta_{N}(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})).

Proof.

For simplicity, we will omit
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
from the notation for
M^{\beta}_{N}
for this proof. By changing to the basis of
\mathcal{P}_{N}
given by orthogonal polynomials, we see that
\det(M_{N}^{2})=\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\kappa_{i}^{2}
. In addition, we see that if we define for
1\leq i,j\leq N
,

(2.32)

[M^{{}^{\prime}1}_{N}]_{ij}=\int(J_{N}^{-1}p_{i-1})(x)p_{j-1}(x)w_{N}(x)dx,\qquad[M^{{}^{\prime}4}_{N}]_{ij}=\int(J_{N}p_{i-1})(x)p_{j-1}(x)w_{N}(x)dx,

then by again changing the basis, we have that

(2.33)

\det(M^{1}_{2N})\det(M^{4}_{N})=(\prod_{i=0}^{N-1}\kappa_{i}^{2})^{2}\det(M^{{}^{\prime}1}_{N})\det(M^{{}^{\prime}4}_{N}).

On the other hand, we see that

(2.34)

[M^{{}^{\prime}1}_{N}(M^{{}^{\prime}4}_{N})^{T}]_{ij}=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}(\int(J_{N}^{-1}p_{i-1})(x)p_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx)(\int(J_{N}p_{j-1})(y)p_{k}(y)w_{N}(y)dy)=

(2.35)

\int(J_{N}^{-1}p_{i-1})(x)(\int K_{N}(x,y)(J_{N}p_{j-1})(y)w_{N}(y)dy)w_{N}(x)dx=

(2.36)

\int(J_{N}^{-1}p_{i-1})(x)(\Pi_{N}J_{N}p_{j-1})(x)w_{N}(x)dx=-\int p_{i-1}(x)(J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}J_{N}p_{j-1})(x)w_{N}(x)dx.

In particular

(2.37)

\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{pf}(M^{1}_{N})\mathrm{pf}(M^{4}_{N})}{\det(M^{2}_{N})}\bigg]^{2}=\det(M^{{}^{\prime}1}_{N})\det(M^{{}^{\prime}4}_{N})=(-1)^{N}\det(\Delta_{N}).

As
N
is even, this completes the proof.
∎

These lemmas combined complete the verification of (
1.25
). To proceed, we must reduce
\Delta_{N}
to a more manageable form. In view of the identity,

(2.38)

\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}J_{N}\Pi_{N}=\Pi_{N}-\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}[J_{N},\Pi_{N}]\Pi_{N}

we see that it suffices to understand the action of
J_{N}^{-1}[J_{N},\Pi_{N}]
on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. To do so, we first compute the commutator of
[J_{N},\Pi_{N}]
on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. For this, we recall the Christoffel-Darboux formula (see
[
39
]
):

(2.39)

K_{N}(x,y)=\frac{\kappa_{N-1}}{\kappa_{N}}\left(\frac{p_{N}(x)p_{N-1}(y)-p_{N-1}(x)p_{N}(y)}{x-y}\right);\quad x\neq y,

(2.40)

K_{N}(x,x)=\frac{\kappa_{N-1}}{\kappa_{N}}\left(p_{N}^{\prime}(x)p_{N-1}(x)-p_{N-1}^{\prime}(x)p_{N}(x)\right),

as well as the classical three-term recurrence

(2.41)

xp_{j}(x)=b_{j-1}p_{j-1}(x)+a_{j}p_{j}(x)+b_{j}p_{j+1}(x),\;\;b_{j}=\frac{\kappa_{j}}{\kappa_{j+1}},\;\;a_{j}=\beta_{j}-\beta_{j+1},

where
\beta_{j}
is defined so that
p_{j}(x)\kappa_{j}^{-1}=x^{j}+\beta_{j}x^{j-1}+\cdots
.
Additionally, we denote by

(2.42)

H_{N}(f)(y)=\mathrm{p.v}\int\frac{f(x)}{x-y}w_{N}(x)dx

the
w_{N}
-weighted Hilbert transform of
f
, where here
\mathrm{p.v}
denotes the Cauchy principal value integral taken at
y
. We define, for
1\leq i\leq m
,

(2.43)

\ell_{i}(x)=e^{N\lambda_{i}^{2}}N^{\alpha_{i}}\frac{\kappa_{N-1}}{\kappa_{N}}\frac{p_{N}(x)H_{N}(p_{N-1})(\lambda_{i})-H_{N}(p_{N})(\lambda_{i})p_{N-1}(x)}{x-\lambda_{i}},

(2.44)

q_{i}(x)=e^{-N\lambda_{i}^{2}}N^{-\alpha_{i}}K_{N}(x,\lambda_{i}).

The utility of these functions comes from the following decomposition.

Proposition 2.5
.

For
f\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
, we have that

(2.45)

[J_{N},\Pi_{N}]f=-\sum_{k=1}^{m}e^{-N\lambda_{k}^{2}}N^{-\alpha_{k}}\alpha_{k}\ell_{k}f(\lambda_{k})-2N\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(xf(x))+\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(x)f(x))=

(2.46)

-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}\ell_{k}\int q_{k}(x)f(x)w_{N}(x)dx-2N\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(xf(x))+\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}(x)f(x)).

Proof of Proposition
2.5
.

Observing that
\Pi_{N}f=f
we see that

(2.47)

[J_{N},\Pi_{N}]f=\left[\frac{d}{dx},\Pi_{N}\right]f-2N\left[x,\Pi_{N}\right]f+\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}\left[\frac{1}{(x-\lambda_{k})},\Pi_{N}\right]f+[\mathcal{W}^{\prime},\Pi_{N}]f=

(2.48)

\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(f^{\prime})-2N\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(xf)+\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}f)+\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}\left[\frac{1}{(x-\lambda_{k})},\Pi_{N}\right]f.

We note that
\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(f^{\prime})=0
so it suffices to evaluate
[(x-\lambda_{k})^{-1},\Pi_{N}]
. We compute that

(2.49)

([\frac{1}{(x-\lambda_{k})},\Pi_{N}]f)(x)=

(2.50)

\int(\frac{1}{(x-\lambda_{k})}-\frac{1}{(y-\lambda_{k})})\frac{k_{N-1}}{k_{N}}\frac{p_{N}(x)p_{N-1}(y)-p_{N}(y)p_{N-1}(x)}{(x-y)}f(y)w_{N}(y)dy=

(2.51)

-\int\frac{k_{N-1}}{k_{N}}\frac{p_{N}(x)p_{N-1}(y)-p_{N}(y)p_{N-1}(x)}{(x-\lambda_{k})(y-\lambda_{k})}f(y)w_{N}(y)dy=

(2.52)

-\int\frac{k_{N-1}}{k_{N}}\frac{p_{N}(x)p_{N-1}(y)-p_{N}(y)p_{N-1}(x)}{(x-\lambda_{k})}(\frac{f(y)-f(\lambda_{k})}{y-\lambda_{k}}+\frac{f(\lambda_{k})}{y-\lambda_{k}})w_{N}(y)dy=

(2.53)

-\int\frac{k_{N-1}}{k_{N}}\frac{p_{N}(x)p_{N-1}(y)-p_{N}(y)p_{N-1}(x)}{(x-\lambda_{k})(y-\lambda_{k})}f(\lambda_{k})w_{N}(y)dy=-e^{-N\lambda_{k}^{2}}N^{-\alpha_{k}}\ell_{k}(x)f(\lambda_{k}),

where in second to last equality we have used that fact that
(f(y)-f(\lambda_{k}))/(y-\lambda_{k})
is a polynomial of degree less than
N-1
, so that

(2.54)

\int p_{N}(y)\frac{f(y)-f(\lambda_{k})}{y-\lambda_{k}}w_{N}(y)dy=\int p_{N-1}(y)\frac{f(y)-f(\lambda_{k})}{y-\lambda_{k}}w_{N}(y)dy=0.

Together, these statements establish the first equality. To obtain the second equality, we observe that

(2.55)

\int q_{i}(x)f(y)w_{N}(x)dx=e^{-N\lambda_{i}^{2}}N^{-\alpha_{i}}f(\lambda_{i}).

∎

We now introduce a modification of
\Delta_{N}
that will be more computable. For
f\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
let

(2.56)

\Delta_{N}^{0}f=f+\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}(\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}\ell_{k}\int q_{k}(y)f(y)w_{N}(y)dy+2N\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(xf(x))-\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}(x)f(x)))

and let us denote the difference

(2.57)

\Delta_{N}^{1}=\Delta_{N}-\Delta_{N}^{0}=-\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\mathcal{E}^{\prime},

where
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W}_{0}
, as before. It is clear that when
\mathcal{W}=0
, we have that
\Delta_{N}^{0}=\Delta_{N}
. In general, we shall show that this difference is negligible on the exponential scale.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
be as in Theorem
1.2
. There exists a choice of
c>0
such that

(2.58)

\det(\Delta_{N})=\det(\Delta_{N}^{0})+O(e^{-Nc}).

Moreover for any choice of compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
, we may choose
c>0
small enough that the error term is uniform over the chosen of compact subset.

This proposition will be proven in Section
5
. We now turn our attention to the variant
\Delta_{N}^{0}
. Let us write
d=\max(1,\deg(\mathcal{W}_{0})-1)
, and let us define two subspaces of
\mathcal{P}_{N}
:

(2.59)

\mathcal{P}_{N,2}=\{f\in\mathcal{P}_{N-d}:f(\lambda_{1})=\cdots=f(\lambda_{m})=0\},

and
\mathcal{P}_{N,1}=\mathcal{P}_{N,2}^{\perp}
, the subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{N}
which is orthogonal to
\mathcal{P}_{N,2}
with respect to the inner-product product of
L^{2}(w_{N})
. We first show a preliminary result about these subspaces.

Lemma 2.7
.

If
N>m+d+1
, then
\mathcal{B}_{N}=(q_{1},\cdots,q_{m},p_{N-d},\cdots,p_{N-1})
is a basis of
\mathcal{P}_{N,1}
. Additionally for
f\in\mathcal{P}_{N,2}
, we have that
\Delta_{N}^{0}f=f
.

Proof.

By the Euclidean algorithm for polynomials, we conclude that
\dim(\mathcal{P}_{N,2})=N-m-d
, so that
\dim(\mathcal{P}_{N,1})=m+d
. In view of (
2.55
) we see that
\mathcal{B}_{N}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{N,1}
. Thus to show that
\mathcal{B}_{N}
is a basis for
\mathcal{P}_{N,1}
, it suffices to show that it is linearly independent. For this, we observe that for
1\leq i,j\leq m
, and
1\leq l\leq d

(2.60)

\int q_{i}(x)\prod_{k=1,k\neq j}^{m}(x-\lambda_{k})w_{N}(x)dx=\delta_{ij}e^{-N\lambda_{i}^{2}}N^{-\alpha_{i}}\prod_{k=1,k\neq j}^{m}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k}),

(2.61)

\int p_{N-l}(x)\prod_{k=1,k\neq j}^{m}(x-\lambda_{k})w_{N}(x)dx=0.

From the first relation we observe that
(q_{1},\cdots q_{m})
is linearly independent, and when combined with the second we see that

(2.62)

\mathrm{span}(q_{1},\cdots q_{m})\cap\mathrm{span}(p_{N-d},\cdots,p_{N-1})=\{0\}.

As the set
(p_{N-d},\cdots p_{N-1})
is linearly independent by construction, these statements combined prove the linear independence of
\mathcal{B}_{N}
, which completes the proof of the first claim.

To demonstrate the second claim, we observe that if
f\in\mathcal{P}_{N,2}
, then
(2Nx-\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}(x))f(x)\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
, so
\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(2Nx-\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}(x))f(x)=0
. We have as well that

(2.63)

\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}\ell_{k}\int q_{k}(y)f(y)w_{N}(y)dy=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}\ell_{k}e^{-N\lambda_{k}^{2}}N^{-\alpha_{k}}f(\lambda_{k})=0,

so indeed, we see that for
f\in\mathcal{P}_{N,2}
, only the identity term is nonzero, which completes the proof of the second claim.
∎

Let us now write the block-decomposition of the operator
\Delta_{N}^{0}
on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
with respect to
(\mathcal{P}_{N,1},\mathcal{P}_{N,2})
as

(2.64)

\Delta_{N}^{0}=\begin{bmatrix}[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{11}&[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{12}\\
[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{21}&[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{22}\end{bmatrix},

and similarly for other operators on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. From the second claim of Lemma
2.7
, we see that
[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{12}=0
and
[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{22}=I
. In particular, we have that
\det(\Delta_{N}^{0})=\det([\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{11})
.

We now focus our attention on understanding
\det([\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{11})
. To proceed we observe that for
f\in\mathcal{P}_{N}
, we have that
(2Nx-\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}(x))f(x)\in\mathcal{P}_{N+d}
. Noting that for
g\in\mathcal{P}_{N+d}
,

(2.65)

(\Pi_{N}^{\perp}g)(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{N+i-1}(x)\int p_{N+i-1}(y)g(y)w_{N}(y)dy,

we see that

(2.66)

(\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(2Ny-\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}(y))f(y))(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{N+i-1}(x)\int p_{N+i-1}(y)(2Ny-\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}(y))f(y)w_{N}(y)dy=

(2.67)

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}p_{N+i-1}(x)\int(2Nz-\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}(z))p_{N+i-1}(z)p_{k}(z)w_{N}(z)dz\int p_{k}(y)f(y)w_{N}(y)dy=

(2.68)

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{N+i-1}(x)\int(2Nz-\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{0}(z))p_{N+i-1}(z)p_{N-j}(z)w_{N}(z)dz\int p_{N-j}(y)f(y)w_{N}(y)dy,

where we have employed the relation
\Pi_{N}f=f
in the second equality, and in the third we have observed that
(2Nz-\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}(z))p_{k}(z)\in\mathcal{P}_{k+d+1}
so that if
k<N-d
and
i>0

(2.69)

\int(2Nx-\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}(x))p_{N+i-1}(x)p_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=0.

If we define
d
-by-
d
matrices
M_{N,0},M_{N,1}
by setting for
1\leq i,j\leq d
,

(2.70)

[M_{N,0}]_{ij}=\int 2Nzp_{N+i-1}(z)p_{N-j}(z)w_{N}(z)dz,\;\;[M_{N,1}]_{ij}=-\int\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}(z)p_{N+i-1}(z)p_{N-j}(z)w_{N}(z)dz,

then we may rewrite (
2.68
) as

(2.71)

\Pi_{N}^{\perp}(2Ny-\mathcal{W}_{0}^{\prime}(y)f(y))(x)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}[M_{N,0}+M_{N,1}]_{ij}p_{N+i-1}(x)\int p_{N-j}(y)f(y)w_{N}(y)dy.

To understand the other terms in
\Delta_{N}^{0}
we further define an
(d+m)
-by-
(d+m)
matrix,
M_{N}
, by

(2.72)

M_{N}=\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c|ccc@{}}M_{N,0}+M_{N,1}&&&\\
\hline\cr&\alpha_{1}&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\alpha_{m}\end{array}\right),

and define an
(d+m)
-by-
(d+m)
inner-product matrix by

(2.73)

D_{N}=\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c|c@{}}\int J_{N}^{-1}p_{N+j-1}(x)p_{N-i}(x)w_{N}(x)dx&\int J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{l}(x)p_{N-i}(x)w_{N}(x)dx\\
\hline\cr\int J_{N}^{-1}p_{N+j-1}(x)q_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx&\int J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{l}(x)q_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx\\
\end{array}\right)

where
1\leq i,j\leq d
and
1\leq k,l\leq m
. We now have the following identification.

Lemma 2.8
.

For
N>d+m+1
, we have that
\det(\Delta^{0}_{N})=\det(I+D_{N}M_{N})
.

Proof.

Let us denote
v_{i}=p_{N-i}
for
1\leq i\leq d
and
v_{i}=q_{i-d}
for
d<i\leq m+d
. By Lemma
2.7
this is a basis for
\mathcal{P}_{N,1}
. We shall denote the matrix for the operator
[\Delta^{0}_{N}]_{11}
with respect to this basis as
\Delta_{N,\mathcal{B}}^{0}
, so that by definition
\det([\Delta^{0}_{N}]_{11})=\det(\Delta_{N,\mathcal{B}}^{0})
. We will also denote
u_{i}=p_{N+i-1}
for
1\leq i\leq d
, and
u_{i}=\ell_{i-d}
for
d<i\leq m+d
. Now with (
2.71
), and recalling the notation
(f,g)_{w,N}=\int f(x)g(x)w_{N}(x)dx
, we see that for
f\in\mathcal{P}_{N}

(2.74)

\Delta_{N}^{0}f=f+\sum_{i,j=1}^{d+m}(\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}u_{i})M_{ij}(v_{j},f)_{w,N}.

Let us define an
(m+d)
-by-
(m+d)
matrix
O
by setting for
1\leq i,j\leq m+d
,
O_{ij}=(v_{j},v_{i})_{w,N}
. This matrix is invertible as
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{d+m}
is a linearly independent set. We also observe that

(2.75)

(\Delta^{0}_{N}v_{j},v_{i})_{w,N}=([\Delta^{0}_{N}]_{11}v_{j},v_{i})_{w,N}=\sum_{k=1}^{d+m}([\Delta^{0}_{N,\mathcal{B}}]_{kj}v_{k},v_{i})_{w,N}=[O\Delta_{N,\mathcal{B}}^{0}]_{ij}.

On the other hand noting that
[D_{N}]_{ij}=(J^{-1}_{N}u_{j},v_{i})_{w,N}
, we by (
2.74
) that

(2.76)

(\Delta^{0}_{N}v_{j},v_{i})_{w,N}=O_{ij}+(D_{N}M_{N}O)_{ij},

so that finally

(2.77)

\Delta_{N,\mathcal{B}}^{0}=O^{-1}(I+D_{N}M_{N})O.

Taking the determinant of both sides completes the proof.
∎

We will later derive that (see Lemma
4.5
) for fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that
b_{N-k},a_{N-k}=O(1)
. Repeatedly applying (
2.41
) we may thus conclude that for
1\leq i,j\leq m+d
,
[M_{N,1}]_{ij}=O(1)
and
[M_{N,0}]_{ij}=2Nb_{N-1}\delta_{ij}\delta_{j1}=O(N)\delta_{ij}\delta_{j1}
. Our understanding of
D_{N}
comes from the following proposition.

Proposition 2.9
.

For
N
even,
1\leq l,k\leq m
, and
1\leq i,j\leq d
we have that

(2.78)

\int J_{N}^{-1}p_{N+j-1}(x)p_{N-i}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=O(N^{-1}),\;\;\;\int J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{l}(x)p_{N-i}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=O(N^{-1}\log(N)),

(2.79)

\int J_{N}^{-1}p_{N+j-1}(x)q_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=O(N^{-1/2}),

(2.80)

\int J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{l}(x)q_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=O(N^{-1/2}),\;\;\;\int J_{N}^{-1}p_{N}(x)p_{N-1}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=O(N^{-7/6}).

Moreover each error term is uniform over compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
.

The proof of this will be carried out in Section
6
. We are now ready to give the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

As noted above, it suffices to show that
\det(\Delta_{N})=1+O(N^{-1/6})
. By Proposition
2.6
, we see that it further suffices to show that
\det(\Delta^{0}_{N})=\det(I+D_{N}M_{N})=1+O(N^{-1/6})
. If we write
D_{N}
as a block matrix with square blocks of size
1
by
(d-1)
by
m
, we see that Proposition
2.9

shows that

(2.81)

D_{N}=\begin{bmatrix}O(N^{-7/6})&O(N^{-1})&O(N^{-1}\log(N))\\
O(N^{-1})&O(N^{-1})&O(N^{-1}\log(N))\\
O(N^{-1/2})&O(N^{-1/2})&O(N^{-1/2})\\
\end{bmatrix},

where all errors are taken entrywise in each block. Similarly, the above observations for
M_{N}
show that

(2.82)

M_{N}=\begin{bmatrix}O(N)&O(1)&0\\
O(1)&O(1)&0\\
0&0&O(1)\\
\end{bmatrix}.

In particular, we see that

(2.83)

I+D_{N}M_{N}=\begin{bmatrix}1+O(N^{-1/6})&O(N^{-1})&O(N^{-1}\log(N))\\
O(1)&I+O(N^{-1})&O(N^{-1}\log(N))\\
O(N^{1/2})&O(N^{-1/2})&I+O(N^{-1/2})\\
\end{bmatrix}.

If we denote the diagonal matrix
\Lambda_{N}={\rm{diag}}(N^{-2/3},1,\cdots,1)
we see that

(2.84)

\Lambda_{N}^{-1}(I+D_{N}M_{N})\Lambda_{N}=I+\Lambda_{N}^{-1}D_{N}M_{N}\Lambda_{N}=\begin{bmatrix}1+O(N^{-1/6})&O(N^{-1/3})&O(N^{-1/3}\log(N))\\
O(N^{-2/3})&I+O(N^{-1})&O(N^{-1}\log(N))\\
O(N^{-1/6})&O(N^{-1/2})&I+O(N^{-1/2})\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Taking the determinant of this expression we see that

(2.85)

\det(I+D_{N}M_{N})=\det(\Lambda_{N}^{-1}(I+D_{N}M_{N})\Lambda_{N})=1+O(N^{-1/6}).

Noting as well that all of the error estimates we have used are uniform in the sense of Theorem
1.2
, we obtain the desired statement.
∎

2.1
Case of Merging Singularities

As mentioned above, to prove Theorem
1.1
, we will need to supplement Theorem
1.2
with an analogous result in the case in which
m=2
,
\mathcal{W}=0
, and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha
, but where
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
are allowed to depend on
N
and
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}\to 0
. We will assume this set-up for the remainder of this subsection. We have the following analog of Theorem
1.2
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let us fix a choice
\alpha>0
,
\epsilon>0
, and
0<\gamma<1
. Then for any
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon)
(possibly
N
-dependant) such that
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})>N^{-\gamma}
, we have that

(2.86)

\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{2}|D_{2N,1}(\lambda_{i})|^{\alpha}]\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{2}|D_{N,4}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha}]=\mathbb{E}[\prod_{i=1}^{2}|D_{2N,2}(\lambda_{i})|^{2\alpha}](1+O(\max(N^{-1/6},N^{\gamma-1}\log(N)))).

Moreover the error term is uniform over all available choices of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon)
with
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})>N^{-\gamma}
.

Remark 2.11
.

We observe that as we have allowed the choice of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
to be
N
-dependant, the uniformity of the error claimed in Proposition
2.10
follows immediately from the pointwise statement of (
2.86
).

Remark 2.12
.

We note that the case of
m=2
and
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})=0
coincides with Theorem
1.2
in the case of
m=1
with
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{1}
and
\alpha\mapsto 2\alpha
. In particular, by Theorem
1.2
,
|\det(\Delta_{N})|=1+O(N^{-1/6})
. As such, we expect that the apparent divergence of the error-term in Proposition
2.10
as
N(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})=O(1)
is an artifact of our method of proof, which avoids using more refined asymptotics for the singularities in the merging case.

All of the constructions of the above section work without modification. We also note that an analog of Proposition
2.6
is not required as
\mathcal{W}=0
in this case. We will show in Proposition
4.16
below, that the conclusions of Lemma
4.5
still hold, so that
[M_{N,0}]_{11}=2Nb_{N-1}=O(N)
uniformly in the choice of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
. We also have that
M_{N,1}=0
. Finally, we will need the following modification of Proposition
2.9
.

Proposition 2.13
.

With the assumptions of Proposition
2.10
and
N
even, we have for
1\leq l,k\leq 2
that

(2.87)

\int J_{N}^{-1}p_{N}(x)p_{N-1}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=O(N^{-1-\min(1-\gamma,1/6)}),

(2.88)

\int J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{l}(x)p_{N-1}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|^{\alpha}O(\max(N^{-1/2-(1-\gamma)},N^{-1}\log(N))),

(2.89)

\int J_{N}^{-1}p_{N}(x)q_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|^{-\alpha}O(N^{-1/2}),

(2.90)

\int J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{l}(x)q_{k}(x)w_{N}(x)dx=O(\max(N^{-1/2},N^{\gamma-1}\log(N))).

Moreover all error terms are uniform over all available choices of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon)
with
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})>N^{-\gamma}
.

The proof of this result will be given in Section
7
.

Proof of Proposition
2.10
.

As above, it will suffice to show that

(2.91)

\det(I+D_{N}M_{N})=1+O(N^{-\min(1-\gamma,1/6)}\log(N)).

If we let
\delta=\min(1-\gamma,1/6)
and define the diagonal matrix
\Lambda_{N}={\rm{diag}}(N^{-1/2-\delta}|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|^{\alpha},1,1)
, then as above, Proposition
2.13
shows that when we write the
3
-by-
3
matrix
\Lambda_{N}^{-1}D_{N}M_{N}\Lambda_{N}
in terms of square blocks of size
1
and
2
we have that

(2.92)

I+\Lambda_{N}^{-1}D_{N}M_{N}\Lambda_{N}=\begin{bmatrix}1+O(N^{-\delta})&O(\max(N^{-(1-\gamma)+\delta},N^{-1/2+\delta}\log(N)))\\
O(N^{-\delta})&I+O(\max(N^{-1/2},N^{\gamma-1}\log(N)))\end{bmatrix}.

We observe that
\max(N^{-(1-\gamma)+\delta},N^{-1/2+\delta}\log(N))\leq 1
and that
\max(N^{-1/2},N^{\gamma-1}\log(N))\leq N^{-\delta}\log(N)
so that (
2.92
) implies the weaker bound

(2.93)

I+\Lambda_{N}^{-1}D_{N}M_{N}\Lambda_{N}=\begin{bmatrix}1+O(N^{-\delta})&O(1)\\
O(N^{-\delta})&I+O(N^{-\delta}\log(N))\end{bmatrix}.

Taking a cofactor expansion in the first column we see that

(2.94)

\det(I+\Lambda_{N}^{-1}D_{N}M_{N}\Lambda_{N})=1+O(N^{-\delta}\log(N)),

implies the desired claim.
∎

3
Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section, we will complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
given in the introduction, assuming Theorem
1.2
and Proposition
2.10
. As explained in the introduction, it will suffice to show that the quantity

(3.1)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)-\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}

coincides with the quantity

(3.2)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\mu_{2N,2,2\alpha}(\varphi)-\mu_{2N,M,2,2\alpha}(\varphi))^{2}

which vanishes for
0<2\alpha<\sqrt{2}
by Proposition 2.9 of
[
4
]
. We begin by noting that by classical asymptotics for smooth, compactly-supported linear statistics (see
[
26
]
), we have that

(3.3)

\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi)^{2})=\lim_{M\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\mu_{2N,M,2,2\alpha}(\varphi)^{2}).

For the remaining terms, we have that

(3.4)

\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)^{2})=\int\int\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{\alpha}|D_{2N,1}(y)|^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(x)|^{2\alpha}|D_{N,4}(y)|^{2\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(y)|^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(y)|^{2\alpha}}dxdy.

(3.5)

\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi))=\int\int\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{\alpha}e^{\alpha X_{2N,M,1}(y)}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(x)|^{2\alpha}e^{2\alpha X_{N,M,4}(y)}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}e^{\alpha X_{2N,M,1}(y)}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}e^{2\alpha X_{N,M,4}(y)}}dxdy.

Working first with the latter, we may apply Theorem
1.2
to the integrand of (
3.5
) to obtain that

(3.6)

\mathbb{E}(\nu_{N,\alpha}(\varphi)\nu_{N,M,\alpha}(\varphi))=\int\int\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}e^{2\alpha X_{2N,M,2}(y)}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}e^{2\alpha X_{2N,M,2}(y)}}(1+O(N^{-1/6}))dxdy=

(3.7)

\int\int\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}e^{2\alpha X_{2N,M,2}(y)}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}e^{2\alpha X_{2N,M,2}(y)}}dxdy(1+O(N^{-1/6}))=

(3.8)

\mathbb{E}(\mu_{2N,2,2\alpha}(\varphi)\mu_{2N,M,2,2\alpha}(\varphi))(1+O(N^{-1/6})),

where we have used the uniformity of the
O(N^{-1/6})
-term to obtain the second equality. Thus we are left to understand (
3.4
). This case is complicated by the non-uniformity of the error of Theorem
1.2
around the diagonal. We will proceed similarly to the proof of Proposition 6.4 of
[
4
]
. For any
\epsilon>0
and
\beta\in(2\alpha^{2},1)
, we may decompose the integral of (
3.4
) into three integrals over
\{|x-y|>\epsilon\}
,
\{\epsilon>|x-y|>2N^{-\beta}\}
, and
\{2N^{-\beta}>|x-y|\}
. We denote these integrals by
A_{1,\epsilon}^{N}
,
A_{2,\epsilon}^{N}
and
A_{3}^{N}
, respectively. Identically to the above case, we can show that for fixed
\epsilon>0

(3.9)

A_{1,\epsilon}^{N}=\int\int_{|x-y|>\epsilon}\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}dxdy(1+O(N^{-1/6})).

In view of the proof of Proposition 6.4 of
[
4
]
, we have that

(3.10)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}A_{1,\epsilon}^{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\mu_{2N,2,2\alpha}(\varphi)^{2}),

so that it suffices to show that
\lim_{N\to\infty}A_{3}^{N}=0
and that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{2,\epsilon}^{N}=0
.
To bound
A_{3}^{N}
, we observe by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we have that

(3.11)

A_{3}^{N}\leq\int\int_{2N^{-\beta}>|x-y|}\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(x)|^{4\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(y)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(y)|^{4\alpha}}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(x)|^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,1}(y)|^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{N,4}(y)|^{2\alpha}}dxdy.

By repeatedly applying Theorem
1.2
we may write the right-hand side as

(3.12)

\int\int_{2N^{-\beta}>|x-y|}\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{4\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{4\alpha}}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}dxdy(1+O(N^{-1/6})).

By applying the asymptotics of
[
30
]
(see also the proof of Proposition 6.4 in
[
4
]
) we see that

(3.13)

\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{4\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{4\alpha}}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}=O(N^{2\alpha^{2}-\beta}),

uniformly on the support of
\varphi
. As
\beta>2\alpha^{2}
,
we see that
\lim_{N\to\infty}A_{3}^{N}=0
.

We finally show that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{2,\epsilon}^{N}=0
. By repeatedly applying Proposition
2.10
as before, we have that

(3.14)

A_{2,\epsilon}^{N}=\int\int_{2N^{-\beta}<|x-y|<\epsilon}\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}dxdy(1+O(N^{-\min(1-\beta,1/6)}\log(N))).

It is shown in the last line of the proof of Proposition 6.4 of
[
4
]
that

(3.15)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\int\int_{2N^{-\beta}<|x-y|<\epsilon}\varphi(x)\varphi(y)\frac{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}{\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(x)|^{2\alpha}\mathbb{E}|D_{2N,2}(y)|^{2\alpha}}dxdy=0.

As
\beta<1
, together these imply that
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}A_{2,\epsilon}^{N}=0
, which completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

4
Asymptotics of Orthogonal Polynomials and Related Quantities

In this section, we state the leading order asymptotics for orthogonal polynomials with respect to the measure
w_{N}
, as well as those for some related quantities. Due to the complexity of the expressions of this asymptotics (especially in the regions around
\lambda_{i}
), we will state our expressions as entries in a matrix product. These asymptotics were obtained in our setting by
[
4
]
, building upon the work of
[
30
]
. In both cases, they are obtained via asymptotic analysis of an associated Riemann-Hilbert problem.

We note that away from the points
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, these asymptotics are closely related to the classical Plancherel-Rotach asymptotics for Hermite polynomials (see
[
39
]
). In particular, Propositions
4.7
and
4.10
coincide with the corresponding classical expressions for Hermite polynomials except with a modification to the
O(1)
-term. Around the points
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
though (where the asymptotics are described by Proposition
4.13
), they are described in terms of Bessel functions. Some related expressions appear in
[
31
]
, where they give a description of the asymptotics of the Christoffel-Darboux kernel around these points in terms of the Bessel kernel.

The structure of this section will be as follows. We will begin this section by recalling some relevant background and notation to present Proposition
4.2
, which is essentially Theorem 4.37 of
[
4
]
restricted to
\mathbb{R}
. This expression gives (uniform) asymptotics for our desired orthogonal polynomials in terms of a variety of parametric. To make use of these asymptotics, we must recall and compute the behavior of these parametrices on
\mathbb{R}
. The result of this translation will be our main asymptotic results, which are given by Proposition
4.7
,
4.10
, and
4.13
. After this, we will give asymptotics for
\ell_{i}
and
q_{i}
in Proposition
4.14
.

Finally, we will provide the modifications of these results needed in the merging case in Subsection
4.1
. These results will essentially follow from the results of
[
9
,
11
]
. The main results are Propositions
4.16
and
4.17
. As we are only interested in the subcritical merging regime (i.e.,
N(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\to\infty
) our asymptotic expressions will essentially coincide with those in the non-merging case, with the only significant difference being that the bounds on the error terms are weaker, and the domains in which they are valid are smaller.

We will fix for this section a choice of
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
as in Theorem
1.2
. We begin by recalling an analytic matrix-valued function on
\mathbb{C}-\mathbb{R}
,
Y_{N}(z):=Y(z;N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
, defined as

(4.1)

Y_{N}(z)=\begin{bmatrix}\kappa_{N}^{-1}p_{N}(z)&\kappa_{N}^{-1}(2\pi i)^{-1}\int\frac{p_{N}(y)}{y-z}w_{N}(y)dy\\
-2\pi i\kappa_{N-1}p_{N-1}(z)&-\kappa_{N-1}\int\frac{p_{N-1}(y)}{y-z}w_{N}(y)dy\end{bmatrix},

where
w_{N}
is defined in (
1.26
). This function occurs as the solution to the Riemann-Hilbert problem defined by the measure
w_{N}
, in the sense of
[
16
]
(see Proposition 3.4 of
[
4
]
). The asymptotics of
Y_{N}
are then computed by using the method of nonlinear steepest descent, pioneered in
[
15
]
. Our primary purpose is to specialize these asymptotics to
\mathbb{R}
. To ease the reader in translation of the results of
[
4
]
, we note that we are in their case of
t=s=1
and their
(k,V,\mathcal{T},(x_{i})_{i=1}^{k},(\beta_{i})_{i=1}^{k})
coincides with our
(m,2x^{2},2\mathcal{W},(\lambda_{m-i})_{i=1}^{m},(2\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{m})
. It is well known in the quadratic case that
\ell_{V}=\ell
where
\ell=-1-2\log(2)
, as can easily be verified from (2.3) and (2.4) in
[
4
]
, and that
d_{1}(\lambda)=2/\pi
.

When
x\in\mathbb{R}
we will use the notation
f_{+}(x)=\lim_{z\to x}f(z)
where the limit is taken along sequences in
\mathbb{H}
, within the domain of
f
, not tangential to
\mathbb{R}
. We are interested in understanding the asymptotics of
(Y_{N})_{+}
. The asymptotics of
Y_{N}
are stated in terms of a related matrix-valued function
S_{N}
(defined in (4.5) and (4.11) of
[
4
]
). To understand the relation between
S_{N}
and
Y_{N}
, we recall the
3
rd and
1
st Pauli matrices defined as

(4.2)

\sigma=\sigma_{3}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},\;\;\;\sigma_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}.

Then for
z\in\mathbb{H}
, such that
\mathrm{Re}(z)\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m}\}
, and
\mathrm{Im}(z)
is sufficiently small (depending only on
\mathrm{Re}(z)
), we have that

(4.3)

Y_{N}(z)=e^{N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}S_{N}(z)e^{N(g_{1}(z)-\ell/2)\sigma}\;\;\text{for}\;\;|\mathrm{Re}(z)|\geq 1,

(4.4)

Y_{N}(z)=e^{N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}S_{N}(z)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
f_{1}(z)^{-1}e^{-Nh_{1}(z)}&1\end{bmatrix}e^{N(g_{1}(z)-\ell/2)\sigma}\;\;\text{for}\;\;|\mathrm{Re}(z)|<1,

where
g_{1}(z)
,
h_{1}(z)
and
f_{1}(z)
are defined in (4.3), (4.9) and (4.13) of
[
4
]
. These follow from Definitions 4.1 and 4.9 of
[
4
]
. We will not need the exact form of these functions, though we will need their behavior of
\mathbb{R}
. For this, we define the functions

(4.5)

s(x)=\begin{cases}2\int_{x}^{1}\sqrt{1-y^{2}}dy;\;|x|<1\\
2\int_{1}^{|x|}\sqrt{y^{2}-1}dy;\;|x|\geq 1\end{cases},\;\;\;\omega(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{m}|x-\lambda_{i}|^{2\alpha_{i}}.

We observe as well that

(4.6)

s(x)=\begin{cases}|x|\sqrt{x^{2}-1}-\mathrm{arcosh}(|x|);\;\;|x|\geq 1\\
-x\sqrt{1-x^{2}}+\arccos(x);\;\;|x|<1\end{cases}.

We may now state the behavior of the above functions on the real line.

Lemma 4.1
.

We have that for
x\in\mathbb{R}

(4.7)

(g_{1})_{+}(x)=x^{2}+\ell/2+(h_{1})_{+}(x)/2,\;\;\;(h_{1})_{+}(x)=\begin{cases}-2s(x);\;\;x\geq 1\\
2\pi i-2s(x);\;\;x\leq-1\\
i2s(x);\;\;|x|<1\end{cases},

and
(f_{1})_{+}(x)=e^{2\mathcal{W}(x)}\omega(x)
.

Proof.

The statements for
(h_{1})_{+}
and
(f_{1})_{+}
follow routinely from their definitions (i.e. (4.9) and (4.13) of
[
4
]
). For the results on
(g_{1})_{+}
, combining the results of (4.6) and (4.7) of
[
4
]
, we see that for
x\in(-1,1)
,

(4.8)

(g_{1})_{+}(x)=x^{2}+\ell/2+h_{+}(x)/2=x^{2}+\ell/2+is(x).

Finally, we see by direct computation that for
x\geq 1
,

(4.9)

(g_{1})_{+}(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{2}{\pi}\sqrt{1-\lambda^{2}}\log(x-\lambda)d\lambda=x^{2}+\ell/2-s(x),

and
(g_{1})_{+}(-x)=(g_{1})_{+}(x)+i\pi
.
∎

By applying the results of Lemma
4.1
to (
4.3
-
4.4
) we see that for
x\in(-1,1)\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m}\}

(4.10)

(Y_{N})_{+}(x)=e^{N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}(S_{N})_{+}(x)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
e^{-2Nis(x)-2\mathcal{W}(x)}\omega(x)^{-1}&1\end{bmatrix}e^{Nis(x)\sigma}e^{Nx^{2}\sigma},

and for
|x|\geq 1
that

(4.11)

(Y_{N})_{+}(x)=e^{N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}(S_{N})_{+}(x)e^{-Ns(x)\sigma}e^{Nx^{2}\sigma}(-1)^{NI(x<0)\sigma}.

We are primarily interested in the first column of
Y_{N}
, so noting that
w_{N}(x)=\omega(x)e^{-2Nx^{2}+2\mathcal{W}(x)}
, we see that for
x\in(-1,1)\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m}\}

(4.12)

e^{-N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{11}(x)\\
[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{21}(x)\end{bmatrix}=(S_{N})_{+}(x)\begin{bmatrix}e^{Nis(x)}\\
e^{-Nis(x)-2\mathcal{W}(x)}\omega(x)^{-1}\end{bmatrix}e^{Nx^{2}}=

(4.13)

(S_{N})_{+}(x)e^{Nis(x)\sigma}e^{\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{\sigma/2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{-1/2},

and that for
|x|\geq 1

(4.14)

e^{-N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{11}(x)\\
[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{21}(x)\end{bmatrix}=((S_{N})_{+}(x)e_{1})e^{N(x^{2}-s(x))}(-1)^{NI(x<0)}.

Now Theorem 4.37 of
[
4
]
implies the following asymptotic result, which shows that up to a small error term
R_{N}
,
S_{N}
may be described in terms of various explicit parametrices, whose behavior on
\mathbb{R}
we will specify below. Here and elsewhere, we will use the notation
\lambda_{0}=1
and
\lambda_{m+1}=-1
.

Proposition 4.2
.

There exists
\delta_{0}>0
, such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
, we may write

(4.15)

(S_{N})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))(P_{N})_{+}(x),

for some matrix-valued function
R_{N}(x):=R(x;\delta,N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha\}_{i})
, and

(4.16)

(P_{N})_{+}(x)=\begin{cases}(P_{\lambda_{i}})_{+}(x);\;x\in(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)\\
(P_{\pm 1})_{+}(x);\;x\in(\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta)\\
(P_{\infty})_{+}(x);\;\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

where here
P_{\infty}
,
P_{\lambda_{i}}
, and
P_{\pm 1}
are certain matrix-valued functions defined in Definition 4.12, 4.21, and 4.28 of
[
4
]
, respectively. Then, for any
l\geq 0
, we have that
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly for
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{m+1}\{\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta\}
. Moreover for any choice of compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
, we may choose
\delta>0
small enough that
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly
in both
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\cup_{i=0}^{m+1}\{\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta\}
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in our chosen compact set.

Remark 4.3
.

In the statement of Theorem 4.37 in
[
4
]
, the error bounds are only stated for
R_{N}
and
R^{\prime}_{N}
. On the other hand, the method of proof used to establish the error bounds on
R^{\prime}_{N}
extends trivially to higher derivatives.

We note as well that all of these parametrices are
N
-dependant, though as we will not use them in our final asymptotic expressions, we omit this from the notation. We will compute the values of
P_{\infty}
,
P_{\pm 1}
, and
P_{\lambda_{i}}
on
\mathbb{R}
below, though before beginning this computation we explain how to obtain results for the full range of polynomials
p_{N-k}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
fixed from Proposition
4.2
instead of only
(p_{N},p_{N-1})
. As the polynomials
p_{N-k}(x):=p_{N-k}(x;N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
themselves depend on the
N
-dependant measure
w_{N}
, we observe that the
(N-k)
-case of Proposition
4.2
does not precisely describe an asymptotic for
p_{N-k}
. On the other hand, we observe that if we define
\eta_{k}=\eta_{N,k}=\sqrt{(N-k)/N}
, then for
N>k
, and
\mathcal{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}
, the polynomial

(4.17)

\eta_{k}^{\mathcal{A}+1/2}p_{N-k}(\eta_{k}x;N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})

coincides with the
(N-k)
-th orthogonal polynomial with respect to the rescaled measure

(4.18)

w_{N,k}(x)=w_{N,k}(x;\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=w_{N}(\eta x)\eta^{-2\mathcal{A}}=e^{2\mathcal{W}(\eta_{k}x)}e^{-2(N-k)x^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}|x-\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}|^{2\alpha_{i}}.

If we denote
\mathcal{W}_{N,k}(x)=\mathcal{W}(\eta_{k}x)
, then we see that

(4.19)

w_{N,k}(x;\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=w_{N-k}(x;\mathcal{W}_{N,k},\{\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}).

In particular, we may apply Proposition
4.2
to
w_{N,k}
to obtain asymptotics for
p_{N-k}
defined with respect to
w_{N}
, though with a number of scaling factors of
\eta_{k}
appearing. For clarity of the notation, we will reserve the notations
P_{\lambda_{i}}
,
P_{\infty}
,
P_{\pm 1}
for the parametrices resulting from the choice of parameters
(N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
and will take care to explicitly specify when objects are considered which are defined with respect to the measure
w_{N,k}
.

Lastly, to account for the factors of
\eta_{k}
appearing in the following asymptotics, we will have repeated use for the following result, which follows from an application of Taylor’s Theorem.

Lemma 4.4
.

For fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define
s_{N,k}(x)=s(\eta_{k}^{-1}x)
. Then if one defined functions
r_{N}
such that

(4.20)

(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)=Ns(x)-k\mathrm{arccos}(x)+r_{N}(x)\;\;\text{for}\;\;|x|<1,

(4.21)

(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)=Ns(x)+k\mathrm{arcosh}(|x|)+r_{N}(x)\;\;\text{for}\;\;|x|>1,

then for any
l\geq 0
, we have that
r_{N}^{(l)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly over compact subsets of
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
.

We are now ready to begin stating our asymptotic expressions, beginning with those for some leading-order coefficients of
p_{N-k}
specified in (
2.41
).

Lemma 4.5
.

For fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that

(4.22)

\kappa_{N-k}^{2}=\pi^{-1}e^{-(N-k)\ell+k}D_{\infty}^{-2}(1+O(N^{-1})),\;\;\;\;\;\beta_{N+1-k}=O(1),

where here
D_{\infty}=2^{-\mathcal{A}}\exp(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}du)
. Moreover the error terms are uniform over compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
.

Remark 4.6
.

We observe from Lemma
4.5
that

(4.23)

D_{\infty}^{-\sigma}e^{-N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}\kappa_{N}^{-1}p_{N}(x)\\
-2\pi i\kappa_{N-1}p_{N-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}=(I+O(N^{-1}))\begin{bmatrix}\pi^{1/2}p_{N}(x)\\
-i\pi^{1/2}p_{N-1}(x)\end{bmatrix},

where the error term in (
4.23
) is independent of
x
and uniform in
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in the same sense as Lemma
4.5
.

For notational clarity, we will delay the proof of Lemma
4.5
to after the statement of Proposition
4.7
below. Now we define functions

(4.24)

D(x)=\begin{cases}\exp(i[-\mathcal{A}\mathrm{arccos}(x)+\frac{\sqrt{1-x^{2}}}{\pi}\mathrm{p.v}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}\frac{1}{x-u}du]);\;\;\;|x|<1\\
\exp([-\mathcal{A}\mathrm{arcosh}(x)+\frac{\sqrt{x^{2}-1}}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}\frac{1}{x-u}du]);\;\;\;x>1\\
\exp([-\mathcal{A}\mathrm{arcosh}(-x)-\frac{\sqrt{x^{2}-1}}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}\frac{1}{x-u}du]);\;\;\;x<-1\end{cases}.

We introduce a fixed matrix-valued function
A(x)
, defined for
|x|<1
by

(4.25)

A(x)=\frac{1}{2(1-x^{2})^{1/4}}\begin{bmatrix}e^{-i\pi/4}\sqrt{1+x}+e^{i\pi/4}\sqrt{1-x}&i[e^{-i\pi/4}\sqrt{1+x}-e^{i\pi/4}\sqrt{1-x}]\\
-i[e^{-i\pi/4}\sqrt{1+x}-e^{i\pi/4}\sqrt{1-x}]&e^{-i\pi/4}\sqrt{1+x}+e^{i\pi/4}\sqrt{1-x}\end{bmatrix},

and on
|x|>1
as

(4.26)

A(x)=\frac{1}{2(x^{2}-1)^{1/4}}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{|1+x|}+\sqrt{|1-x|}&i[\sqrt{|1+x|}-\sqrt{|1-x|}]\\
-i[\sqrt{|1+x|}-\sqrt{|1-x|}]&\sqrt{|1+x|}+\sqrt{|1-x|}\end{bmatrix}.

We now define

(4.27)

T_{\infty}(x)=A(x)D(x)^{-\sigma}.

Lastly we define an additional function

(4.28)

T_{N,k,\infty}(x)=T_{\infty}(\eta_{k}^{-1}x;\mathcal{W}_{N,k},\{\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}).

We may now state the asymptotics away from the points
\{\pm 1,\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m}\}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

For
\delta>0
small enough, and any choice of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we may define
R_{N}(x)
such that for any choice of
0\leq i\leq m
, and
x\in(\lambda_{i+1}+\delta,\lambda_{i}-\delta)
we have that

(4.29)

\begin{bmatrix}\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k}(x)\\
-i\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)e^{[-ik\mathrm{arccos}(x)-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}]\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{iNs(x)}\\
e^{-iNs(x)}\end{bmatrix},

and for
|x|>1+\delta
we have that

(4.30)

\begin{bmatrix}\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k}(x)\\
-i\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{N,k,\infty}(x)e_{1}]e^{-Ns_{N,k}(x)}(-1)^{(N-k)I(x<0)}.

Then for any
l\geq 0
and any choice of compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
, we may choose
\delta_{0}>0
small enough that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
we have that
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in both
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{m+1}(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in our chosen compact set.

In the proof of both of these results, we will need to understand the parametric
P_{\infty}
given in Definition 4.12 of
[
4
]
. While we will not recall the definitions for each of their functions, we will recall some of their real limits for the ease of the reader. Namely the functions
r
,
a
, and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
defined in (4.19), (4.20) and (4.21) satisfy for
x\in\mathbb{R}

(4.31)

r_{+}(x)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{Sign}(x)\sqrt{x^{2}-1};\;\;|x|\geq 1\\
i\sqrt{1-x^{2}};\;\;|x|<1\end{cases},\;\;a_{+}(x)=\frac{|x-1|}{|x+1|}\begin{cases}1\;\;|x|\geq 1\\
e^{i\pi/4};\;\;|x|<1\end{cases},

(4.32)

(\mathcal{D}_{1})_{+}(x)=D(x)\omega(x)^{1/2}\begin{cases}1;\;\;|x|\geq 1\\
e^{\mathcal{W}(x)}e^{-\pi i\sum_{l=1}^{m}\alpha_{l}I(x<\lambda_{l})};\;\;|x|<1\end{cases},

where in the last case for
\mathcal{D}_{1}
we have used the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula: for smooth
f
and
a<0<b
we have that

(4.33)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{f(x)}{x+i\epsilon}dx=-i\pi f(0)+\mathrm{p.v}\int_{a}^{b}\frac{f(x)}{x}dx.

From this we can see that for
x\in(-1,1)\setminus\{\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m}\}
,

(4.34)

(P_{\infty})_{+}(x)=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)e^{-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{m}I(x<\lambda_{k})\alpha_{k}\sigma}e^{-\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2},

and for
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(-1,1)
we have that

(4.35)

(P_{\infty})_{+}(x)=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2}.

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

The proof will be similar to the proof of the
\mathcal{W}=0
case given in Section 4 of
[
30
]
(one may also see the proof of this case given in Section 5 of
[
7
]
). We will first provide a proof in the special case of
k=1
. We observe that we may write

(4.36)

\kappa_{N-1}^{2}=\frac{1}{-2\pi i}\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{21}(x)}{x^{N-1}},\;\;\;\beta_{N}=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{11}(x)-x^{N}}{x^{N-1}}.

Now employing Theorem
4.2
in view of (
4.14
) and (
4.35
), we see that for any small
\delta>0
,
x>1+\delta
, and
j=1,2

(4.37)

e^{N\ell/2(-1)^{j}}[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{j1}(x)=[(I+R_{N}(x))D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2}]_{j1}e^{N(x^{2}-s(x))},

where here
R_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in the sense of Theorem
4.2
. Routine application of Taylor’s Theorem shows that

(4.38)

s(x)=x\sqrt{x^{2}-1}-\mathrm{arcosh}(x)=x^{2}+\ell/2-\log(x)+O(x^{-2}),\;\;\;\omega(x)^{-1/2}=x^{-\mathcal{A}}(1+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}}{x}+O(x^{-2})),

(4.39)

A(x)=I+\frac{1}{2x}\begin{bmatrix}0&i\\
-i&0\end{bmatrix}+O(x^{-2}),\;\;\;D(x)=x^{-\mathcal{A}}D_{\infty}(1+\frac{1}{\pi x}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)u}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}du+O(x^{-2})).

From these results, we see that for
j=1,2
,

(4.40)

[D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2}]_{j1}e^{N(x^{2}-s(x))}=D_{\infty}^{-(-1)^{j}}[A(x)]_{j1}D(x)^{-1}\omega(x)^{-1/2}e^{N(x^{2}-s(x))}=

(4.41)

D_{\infty}^{-(-1)^{j}-1}\bigg[I(1+\frac{1}{x}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)u}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}du\bigg])+\frac{1}{2x}\begin{bmatrix}0&i\\
-i&0\end{bmatrix}+O(x^{-2})\bigg]_{j1}x^{N}e^{-N\frac{\ell}{2}}=

(4.42)

e^{-N\frac{\ell}{2}}D_{\infty}^{-(-1)^{j}-1}\begin{bmatrix}1+x^{-1}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)u}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}du)\\
-x^{-1}i/2\end{bmatrix}_{j}x^{N}+O(x^{N-2}).

We
now recall from (4.58) of
[
4
]
that
R_{N}(x)=O(|x|^{-1})
. In fact, we additionally have that
R_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1}|1+x|^{-1})
, uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
, as in Proposition
4.2
. This is not explicitly stated in
[
4
]
, but follows from a standard contour deformation argument using the uniform decay of the jump matrix (
\Delta=J_{R}-I
in
[
4
]
) for large
|z|
, as in the
\mathcal{W}=0
case discussed in Section 4 of
[
30
]
(one also may consult the detailed argument given in the proof of Proposition 7.5 of
[
10
]
in a related case).

Employing this asymptotic, (
4.42
), and (
4.37
), we see that

(4.43)

\kappa_{N-1}^{2}=\frac{1}{-2\pi i}\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{21}(x)}{x^{N-1}}=\frac{1}{4\pi}e^{-N\ell}D_{\infty}^{-2}(1+O(N^{-1}))=\pi^{-1}e^{-(N-1)\ell+1}D_{\infty}^{-2}(1+O(N^{-1})).

Similarly, we derive that

(4.44)

\beta_{N}=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{[(Y_{N})_{+}]_{11}(x)-x^{N}}{x^{N-1}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}\lambda_{i}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\mathcal{W}(u)u}{\sqrt{1-u^{2}}}du+O(N^{-1})=O(1).

This completes the proof of the
k=1
case. To complete the general case, we see that

(4.45)

\kappa_{N-k-1}(N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=\eta_{k}^{-\mathcal{A}-1/2-(N-k-1)}\kappa_{N-k-1}(N-k,\mathcal{W}_{N,k},\{\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}),

(4.46)

\beta_{N-k}(N,\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=\eta_{k}\beta_{N-k}(N-k,\mathcal{W}_{N,k},\{\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}).

Observing that
\eta_{k}^{-\mathcal{A}-1/2-(N-k-1)}=e^{k/2}(1+O(N^{-1}))
and that
D_{\infty}(\mathcal{W}_{N,k},\{\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=D_{\infty}(1+O(N^{-1}))
, we obtain the general case by applying the
k=1
case to
w_{N,k}
.
∎

Before continuing on to the proof of Proposition
4.7
, we record the following useful remark.

Remark 4.8
.

We observe as
T_{\infty}(x)
is smooth on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
and independent of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
N
, we may show that for fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, if we define
\bar{R}_{N}(x)
so that
T_{N,k,\infty}(x)=T_{\infty}(x)+\bar{R}_{N}(x)
,
then for
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bar{R}_{N}^{(l)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly over both compact subsets of
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
(and of course still independently of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
). On the other hand, taking
R_{N}(x)=\bar{R}_{N}(x)T_{\infty}^{-1}(x)
, we may write

(4.47)

T_{N,k,\infty}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x).

Now as one may check that
\det(T_{\infty}(x))=1
, we see that the entries of
T_{\infty}^{-1}(x)
are also smooth on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
, so we see that
R_{N}
satisfies the same estimates as
\bar{R}_{N}
.

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

We will take
\delta=\delta_{0}/2
, where
\delta_{0}
is the constant given in Proposition
4.2
. We will begin with the proof in the case that
k=0
. By Proposition
4.2
, (
4.34
), and (
4.13
) we see that for
x\in(\lambda_{i+1}+\delta,\lambda_{i}-\delta)

(4.48)

D_{\infty}^{-\sigma}e^{-N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}\kappa_{N}^{-1}p_{N}(x)\\
-2\pi i\kappa_{N-1}p_{N-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=D_{\infty}^{-\sigma}(I+\bar{R}_{N}(x))(P_{\infty})_{+}(x)e^{Nis(x)\sigma}e^{\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{\sigma/2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=

(4.49)

D_{\infty}^{-\sigma}(I+\bar{R}_{N}(x))D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)e^{-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{Nis(x)}\\
e^{-Nis(x)}\end{bmatrix}=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)e^{-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{Nis(x)}\\
e^{-Nis(x)}\end{bmatrix},

where
\bar{R}_{N}(x)
is the error term from Proposition
4.2
and
R_{N}(x)=D_{\infty}^{-\sigma}\bar{R}_{N}(x)D_{\infty}^{\sigma}
. By Remark
4.6
, we see that modifying
R_{N}(x)
by the constant matrix
I+O(N^{-1})
appearing in (
4.23
), we obtain (
4.29
) in the
k=0
case. Now using the fact that the coefficients
D_{\infty}
are
N
-independent and independent of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i}
, we see that the function
R_{N}
satisfies the same estimates as
\bar{R}_{N}
, as stated in Proposition
4.2
. In particular,
R_{N}^{(l)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in the sense needed in Proposition
4.7
. Similarly, using (
4.35
) and (
4.14
) and the same choice of
R_{N}
, we obtain (
4.30
) in the case of
k=0
. Together these complete the
k=0
case.

To establish the case of
k\neq 0
, with notation as above, we see that by Remark
4.8
, Remark
4.6
, and the
k=0
case applied to the measure
w_{N,k}
, we see for
x\in(\lambda_{i+1}+\delta,\lambda_{i}-\delta)
,

Now recalling
r_{N}(x)
from Lemma
4.4
we see that

(4.50)

T_{\infty}(x)e^{-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)}\\
e^{-i(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)}\end{bmatrix}=(I+\hat{R}_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)e^{[-ik\mathrm{arccos}(x)-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}]\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{iNs(x)}\\
e^{-iNs(x)}\end{bmatrix},

where
\hat{R}_{N}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)e^{r_{N}(x)\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)^{-1}-I
. As
r_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
,
T_{\infty}(x)
is smooth on
(-1,1)
, and
\det(T_{\infty}(x))=1
, we see from the estimates on
R_{N}
that
\hat{R}_{N}^{(l)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
for
l\geq 0
. Together, these results complete the proof of (
4.29
). The general case of (
4.30
) follows more simply from Remark
4.6
and a similar appeal to the
k=0
case.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

for any
\delta>0
, we may find
c>0
, such that
s(x)>2cx^{2}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[-1-\delta,1+\delta]
. Noting that
T_{\infty}
grows only polynomially in
x
, we see that the asymptotic (
4.35
) implies that for any choice of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
l\geq 0
, and
|x|>1+\delta
, we have that

(4.51)

p_{N-k}^{(l)}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=O(e^{-Ncx^{2}}),

and additionally, for any compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
, we may choose
c>0
small enough that this error bound is uniform in
|x|>1+\delta
and our chosen compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
.

We will now turn to describe the asymptotics of the orthogonal polynomials around the points
\pm 1
. We define functions

(4.52)

f_{1}(x)=\mathrm{Sign}(x-1)\left(\frac{3}{2}|s(x)|\right)^{2/3},\;\;\;f_{-1}(x)=f_{1}(-x).

It is routine to verify that
f_{\pm 1}
are defined and smooth in a neighborhood around
\pm 1
(see also Definition 4.25 of
[
4
]
). Moreover, one may verify that
f_{\pm 1}^{\prime}(\pm 1)=\pm 2
and that
f_{\pm 1}(\pm 1)=0
. We also define
f_{N,k,\pm 1}(z)=f_{\pm 1}(\eta_{k}^{-1}z)
as before. We will also need additional matrix valued functions

(4.53)

T_{1}(x)=\sqrt{\pi}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}T_{\infty}(x)e^{\mathcal{W}(x)I(x\geq 1)\sigma}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}e^{i\sigma\frac{\pi}{4}I(x<1)}|f_{1}(x)|^{\sigma/4},

(4.54)

T_{-1}(x)=\sqrt{\pi}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}T_{\infty}(x)e^{\mathcal{W}(x)I(x\leq-1)\sigma}e^{-i\pi\mathcal{A}\sigma}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{bmatrix}e^{i\sigma\frac{\pi}{4}I(x>-1)}|f_{-1}(x)|^{\sigma/4}.

We are now ready to state the asymptotics around
\pm 1
. In the following proposition,
\mathrm{Ai}
denotes the Airy function.

Proposition 4.10
.

For
\delta>0
small enough, and any choice of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we may find
R_{N}(x)
such that for
x\in(1-\delta,1+\delta)
we have that

(4.55)

\begin{bmatrix}\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k}(x)\\
-i\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{1}(x)\begin{bmatrix}(N-k)^{1/6}\mathrm{Ai}((N-k)^{2/3}f_{N,k,1}(x))\\
(N-k)^{-1/6}\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}((N-k)^{2/3}f_{N,k,1}(x))\end{bmatrix},

and for
x\in(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)
we have that

(4.56)

\begin{bmatrix}\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k}(x)\\
