-i\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{-1}(x)\begin{bmatrix}(N-k)^{1/6}\mathrm{Ai}((N-k)^{2/3}f_{N,k,-1}(x))\\
-(N-k)^{-1/6}\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}((N-k)^{2/3}f_{N,k,-1}(x))\end{bmatrix}(-1)^{N-k}.

Additionally, for any
l\geq 0
, and any choice of compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
, we may choose
\delta_{0}>0
such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
we have that
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in both
x\in(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)\cup(1-\delta,1+\delta)
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in our chosen compact set.

To prove this, we will have to recall the form of
P_{\pm 1}
. We begin with the case of
P_{1}
, which is given in Definition 4.28 of
[
4
]
. We observe that for the function
\xi_{1}(z)
defined in (4.42) of
[
4
]
, we have that
(\xi_{1})_{+}(x)=N^{2/3}f_{1}(x)
, and additionally we have that the function defined in (4.28) of
[
4
]
has
(\phi_{1})_{+}(x)=(h_{1})_{+}(x)/2
. Employing these observations and Lemma
4.1
we derive from Definition 4.28 of
[
4
]
that for
x\in(1-\delta,1+\delta)

(4.57)

(P_{1})_{+}(x)=F_{+}(x)Q_{+}(Nf_{1}(x))e^{(I(x\geq 1)-iI(x<1))s(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2}e^{-\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma},

(4.58)

F_{+}(x)=(P_{\infty})_{+}(x)e^{\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{\sigma/2}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\sqrt{\pi}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}e^{i\sigma\frac{\pi}{4}I(x<1)}|f_{1}(x)|^{\sigma/4}N^{\sigma/6}e^{-i\pi/12},

(4.59)

Q_{+}(\zeta)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Ai}(\zeta)&\mathrm{Ai}(\omega^{2}\zeta)\\
\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\zeta)&\omega^{2}\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\omega^{2}\zeta)\end{bmatrix}e^{-\pi i\sigma/6};\;\;\zeta>0,

(4.60)

Q_{+}(\zeta)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Ai}(\zeta)&\mathrm{Ai}(\omega^{2}\zeta)\\
\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\zeta)&\omega^{2}\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\omega^{2}\zeta)\end{bmatrix}e^{-\pi i\sigma/6}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{bmatrix};\;\;\zeta<0,

where here
\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}
. Now employing (
4.34
) and (
4.35
) we see that

(4.61)

F_{+}(x)=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)e^{\mathcal{W}(x)I(x\geq 1)}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\sqrt{\pi}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}e^{i\sigma\frac{\pi}{4}I(x<1)}|f_{1}(x)|^{\sigma/4}N^{\sigma/6}e^{-i\pi/12}=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}e^{i\pi/6}T_{1}(x)N^{\sigma/6}.

We now proceed to the
-1
case. While not defined in
[
4
]
, the definition is a similar modification of the equation obtained in the
\mathcal{W}=0
case considered in (80) of
[
30
]
(or similarly, from the
+1
case for the reversed measure
w_{N}(-x,\mathcal{W}(-x),\{-\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
). In particular, we obtain by a similar argument that for
x\in(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)

(4.62)

(P_{-1})_{+}(x)=\hat{F}_{+}(x)\sigma Q_{-}(Nf_{-1}(x))\sigma e^{(I(x\leq-1)-iI(x>-1))s(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2}e^{-\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}(-1)^{N\sigma},

(4.63)

\bar{F}_{+}(x)=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}e^{i\frac{\pi}{6}}T_{-1}(x)N^{\sigma/6},

(4.64)

\sigma Q_{-}(\zeta)\sigma=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Ai}(\zeta)&\omega^{2}\mathrm{Ai}(\omega\zeta)\\
-\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\zeta)&-\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\omega^{2}\zeta)\end{bmatrix}e^{-\pi i\sigma/6};\;\zeta\geq 0,

(4.65)

\sigma Q_{-}(\zeta)\sigma=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Ai}(\zeta)&\omega^{2}\mathrm{Ai}(\omega\zeta)\\
-\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\zeta)&-\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(\omega^{2}\zeta)\end{bmatrix}e^{-\pi i\sigma/6}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
-1&1\end{bmatrix};\;\zeta<0.

Remark 4.11
.

We observe that
F(z)
(
\bar{F}(z)
) is analytic in a neighborhood around
1
(
-1
) respectively (see Lemma 4.30 of
[
4
]
). In particular, from (
4.61
) and (
4.63
) we see that
T_{\pm 1}(z)
is as well. We note that
T_{\pm 1}
is independant of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i}
and
N
. As we also have
|\det(T_{\pm 1}(x))|=4\pi
, the same aurgument as in Remark
4.8
shows that for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is
R_{N}(x)
such that for
x\in(\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta)

(4.66)

T_{\pm 1}(\eta_{k}^{-1}x;\mathcal{W}_{N,k},\{\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\pm 1}(x),

where for any
\ell\geq 0
,
R_{N}^{(\ell)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in both
x\in(\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta)
(and again independently of the choice of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
).

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Proposition
4.7
. In particular, employing Proposition
4.2
, (
4.34
), (
4.13
), (
4.14
) and Remark
4.6
as in the proof of Proposition
4.7
, we obtain the
k=0
case routinely from the expansions for
P_{\pm 1}
given by (
4.57
), (
4.62
), (
4.61
), and (
4.63
). The proof of the arbitrary
k
follows similarly by employing Remark
4.11
.
∎

Finally, we will state the asymptotics in the region around the points
\{\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m}\}
. Define for
x\in(-1,1)
, the function
f_{x}(z)=s(x)-s(z)=2\int_{x}^{z}\sqrt{1-y^{2}}dy
. This function is smooth and invertible in a neighborhood of
x
, and we see that
f_{x}^{\prime}(x)=2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
. As before, we define
f_{N,k,x}(z)=f_{\eta_{k}^{-1}x}(\eta_{k}^{-1}z)
. We define a matrix-valued function for
1\leq j\leq m
by

(4.67)

T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}(x)=e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}T_{\infty}(x)e^{i\frac{\rho_{N,j,k}}{2}\sigma}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{bmatrix},

where
\rho_{N,j,k}
denotes

(4.68)

\rho_{N,j,k}=2(N-k)s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})-\pi\alpha_{j}-2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{i}.

For simplicity, we will denote
T_{N,0,\lambda_{j}}(x)=T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(x)
and
\rho_{N,j,0}=\rho_{N,j}
.

Remark 4.12
.

Unlike
T_{\infty}(x)
and
T_{\pm 1}(x)
, the function
T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}(x)
depends on
N
. On the other hand, this is only through the factor
\exp(i\rho_{N,j,k}\sigma/2)
, whose norm is
N
-independent. Thus by Remark
4.8
we see that
T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}(x)
is smooth on
(-1,1)
, and that for any
\delta>0
, and any
l\geq 0
,
T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}^{(l)}(x)
is uniformly bounded jointly in
N
,
x\in(\lambda_{j}-\delta,\lambda_{j}+\delta)
and any chosen compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in(-1,1)^{m}
. Observing that
|\det(T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}(x))|=1
, we see additionally that the same is true for
T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}^{-1}(x)
. Lastly, combining these observations with Lemma
4.4
, we see that for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is
R_{N}(x)
such that

(4.69)

T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{N,\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{j}}(\eta_{k}^{-1}x;\mathcal{W}_{N,k},\{\eta_{k}^{-1}\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i}),

where for any
l\geq 0
,
R_{N}^{(l)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly both
x\in(\lambda_{j}-\delta,\lambda_{j}+\delta)
and compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in(-1,1)^{m}
.

Lastly, we define the following vector of special functions

(4.70)

\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Sign}(x)\sqrt{\pi|x|}J_{\alpha+1/2}(|x|)\\
\sqrt{\pi|x|}J_{\alpha-1/2}(|x|)\end{bmatrix},

where here
J_{\nu}
denotes the Bessel function of parameter
\nu
.

Proposition 4.13
.

For
\delta>0
small enough, and any choice of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we may find
R_{N}(x)
such that for any choice of
1\leq i\leq m
and
x\in(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)
we have that

(4.71)

\begin{bmatrix}\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k}(x)\\
-i\pi^{1/2}p_{N-k-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=(I+R_{N}(x))T_{N,k,\lambda_{i}}(x)\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}((N-k)f_{N,k,\lambda_{i}}(x)).

Additionally, for any
l\geq 0
, and any choice of compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
, we may choose
\delta_{0}>0
such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
we have that
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in both
x\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}(\lambda_{k}-\delta,\lambda_{k}+\delta)
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
in our chosen compact set.

As before, this result will follow from analyzing the parametrix
P_{\lambda_{j}}
defined in Definition 4.21 of
[
4
]
. We observe that the function defined in (4.27) of
[
4
]
satisfies

(4.72)

(W_{j})_{+}(x)=e^{\mathcal{W}(x)}\omega(x)^{1/2}e^{-i\pi\alpha_{j}\mathrm{Sign}(x-\lambda_{j})}.

Employing this we see that for
x\in(\lambda_{j}-\delta,\lambda_{j}+\delta)

(4.73)

(P_{\lambda_{j}})_{+}(x)=(E_{j})_{+}(x)(\Psi_{\alpha_{j}})_{+}(Nf_{\lambda_{j}}(x))e^{-\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2}e^{i\pi\alpha_{j}\mathrm{Sign}(x-\lambda_{j})\sigma}e^{-Nis(x)\sigma},

(4.74)

(E_{j})_{+}(x)=(P_{\infty})_{+}(x)e^{\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{\sigma/2}e^{-i\pi\alpha_{j}\mathrm{Sign}(x-\lambda_{j})\sigma/2}e^{iNs(\lambda_{j})\sigma}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{bmatrix},

and where
\Psi_{\alpha}
is a fixed matrix-valued function (defined in (4.26-4.33) of
[
40
]
) and satisfying

(4.75)

(\Psi_{\alpha})_{+}(\zeta)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\sqrt{\zeta}\begin{bmatrix}H_{\alpha+1/2}^{(2)}(\zeta)&-iH_{\alpha+1/2}^{(1)}(\zeta)\\
H_{\alpha-1/2}^{(2)}(\zeta)&-iH_{\alpha-1/2}^{(1)}(\zeta)\end{bmatrix}e^{-(\alpha+1/4)\pi i\sigma};\;\;\zeta>0,

(4.76)

(\Psi_{\alpha})_{+}(\zeta)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\sqrt{-\zeta}\begin{bmatrix}iH_{\alpha+1/2}^{(1)}(-\zeta)&-H_{\alpha+1/2}^{(2)}(-\zeta)\\
-iH_{\alpha-1/2}^{(1)}(-\zeta)&H_{\alpha-1/2}^{(2)}(-\zeta)\end{bmatrix}e^{(\alpha+1/4)\pi i\sigma};\;\;\zeta<0,

where here
H^{(1)}_{\beta}
and
H^{(2)}_{\beta}
denote the Hankel functions of the first and second kind at parameter
\beta
, respectively. Employing (
4.34
) we see that we may write

(4.77)

(E_{j})_{+}(x)=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(x).

Proof of Proposition
4.13
.

As we have that
H_{\beta}^{(1)}(x)+H_{\beta}^{(2)}(x)=2J_{\beta}(x)
(see Section 9.1 of
[
1
]
), we see that for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}

(4.78)

(\Psi_{\alpha})_{+}(\zeta)e^{i\pi\alpha\mathrm{Sign}(\zeta)\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=e^{-i\pi/4}\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(\zeta).

Combining this with (
4.73
), (
4.77
), and observing that
\mathrm{Sign}(x-\lambda_{j})=\mathrm{Sign}(Nf_{\lambda_{j}}(x))
, we see that

(4.79)

(P_{\lambda_{j}})_{+}(x)e^{Nis(x)\sigma}e^{\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{\sigma/2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(x)e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}\mathcal{J}_{\alpha_{j}}(Nf_{\lambda_{j}}(x))=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(x)\mathcal{J}_{\alpha_{j}}(Nf_{\lambda_{j}}(x)).

Now employing Proposition
4.2
, (
4.13
), and Remark
4.6
we obtain the
k=0
case of (
4.71
). The proof for arbitrary
k
follows from Remark
4.12
.
∎

Together these results will allow us to uniformly describe the behavior of
p_{N-k}
on
\mathbb{R}
. What remains is to understand the behavior of
\ell_{i}
and
q_{i}
introduced in (
2.43
) and (
2.44
) for
1\leq i\leq m
. To do so, we introduce the following matrix

(4.80)

V_{N,\lambda_{i}}=e^{N\lambda_{i}^{2}\sigma}N^{\alpha_{i}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}-\kappa_{N-1}H(p_{N-1})(\lambda_{i})&-\kappa_{N}^{-1}(2\pi i)^{-1}H(p_{N})(\lambda_{i})\\
2\pi i\kappa_{N-1}p_{N-1}(\lambda_{i})&\kappa^{-1}_{N}p_{N}(\lambda_{i})\end{bmatrix}.

The utility of this matrix comes from the following relation

(4.81)

(x-\lambda_{i})\begin{bmatrix}-\ell_{i}(x)\\
2\pi iq_{i}(x)\end{bmatrix}=V_{N,\lambda_{i}}\begin{bmatrix}\kappa_{N}^{-1}p_{N}(x)\\
-2\pi i\kappa_{N-1}p_{N-1}(x)\end{bmatrix}.

In particular, in view of the above understanding of
p_{N}
and
p_{N-1}
, to understand
\ell_{i}
and
q_{i}
, we only need to provide asymptotics for
V_{N,\lambda_{i}}
.

Proposition 4.14
.

For
1\leq j\leq m
, we have that

(4.82)

V_{N,\lambda_{j}}=c_{j}^{-\sigma}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{4}\sigma}\sigma_{1}T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(\lambda_{j})^{-1}D_{\infty}^{-\sigma}(I+O(N^{-1}))e^{-N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma},

where here we denote

(4.83)

c_{j}=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}(1-\lambda_{j}^{2})^{\alpha_{j}/2}}{\Gamma(\alpha_{j}+1/2)}e^{-\mathcal{W}(\lambda_{j})}\prod_{k\neq j}|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}|^{-\alpha_{k}}.

Moreover the error term is uniform over compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
.

Remark 4.15
.

We observe that by combining Remark
4.6
, Proposition
4.14
, and (
4.81
) we see that for
1\leq i\leq m
there are constants
C_{k,N}
for
1\leq k\leq 4
, such that

(4.84)

\ell_{i}(x)=C_{1,N}\frac{p_{N}(x)}{x-\lambda_{i}}+C_{2,N}\frac{p_{N-1}(x)}{x-\lambda_{i}},

(4.85)

q_{i}(x)=C_{3,N}\frac{p_{N}(x)}{x-\lambda_{i}}+C_{4,N}\frac{p_{N-1}(x)}{x-\lambda_{i}},

where for all
i
, we have that
C_{i,N}=O(1)
uniformly in compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
.

Proof.

By the Christoffel-Darboux formula (
2.39
), we see that
\det(Y_{N}(z))=1
. From this, we observe that for
1\leq j\leq m

(4.86)

V_{N,\lambda_{j}}=e^{N\lambda_{j}^{2}\sigma}N^{\alpha_{j}\sigma}(Y_{N})_{+}(\lambda_{j})^{-1}.

Additionally, we observe the matrix relation

(4.87)

e^{-\mathcal{W}(x)\sigma}\omega(x)^{-\sigma/2}e^{-Nis(x)\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
e^{-2Nis(x)-2\mathcal{W}(x)}\omega(x)^{-1}&1\end{bmatrix}e^{Nis(x)\sigma}e^{Nx^{2}\sigma}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{-\sigma/2}.

Using this relation and Proposition
4.2
, (
4.10
), (
4.73
) and (
4.77
), we see that for
x\in(\lambda_{j},\lambda_{j}+\delta)

(4.88)

(Y_{N})_{+}(x)=e^{N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}(I+R_{N}(x))D_{\infty}^{\sigma}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}}T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(x)(\Psi_{\alpha_{j}})_{+}(Nf_{\lambda_{j}}(x))e^{i\pi\alpha_{j}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}w_{N}(x)^{-\sigma/2},

where
R_{N}
is the remainder term from Proposition
4.2
. We recall from Section 4.3 of
[
40
]
(more precisely, the analytic continuation of their (4.21)) that for
\zeta>0
:

(4.89)

(\Psi_{\alpha})_{+}(\zeta)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
e^{-2\pi i\alpha}&1\end{bmatrix}=\sqrt{\zeta}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\pi}I_{\alpha+1/2}(-i\zeta)&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}K_{\alpha+1/2}(-i\zeta)\\
-i\sqrt{\pi}I_{\alpha-1/2}(-i\zeta)&-\frac{i}{\sqrt{\pi}}K_{\alpha-1/2}(-i\zeta)\end{bmatrix}e^{-i\frac{\alpha}{2}\pi\sigma},

where here
I_{\alpha}
and
K_{\alpha}
are modified Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively, where both are taken with respect to the principal branch given by
\arg(z)\in(-\pi,\pi)
. The asymptotics of the right-hand side of (
4.89
) as
\zeta\to 0
are computed in (112) of
[
30
]
as

(4.90)

e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
-i&0\end{bmatrix}+C_{\alpha}(\zeta)\bigg)\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\zeta^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(\alpha+1/2)2^{\alpha}}\bigg)^{\sigma}e^{-i\pi\alpha\sigma},

where
C_{\alpha}(\zeta)=O(\zeta\log(|\zeta|))
entrywise as
\zeta\to 0
. We note as well that

(4.91)

e^{-i\pi\alpha\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
e^{-2\pi i\alpha}&1\end{bmatrix}^{-1}e^{i\pi\alpha\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}=I.

Recalling that both
R_{N}(x)
and
T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(x)
are continuous at
\lambda_{j}
, we see by (
4.88
) that

(4.92)

(Y_{N})_{+}(\lambda_{j})=e^{N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}(I+R_{N}(\lambda_{j}))D_{\infty}^{\sigma}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(\lambda_{j})e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
-i&0\end{bmatrix}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}(Nf^{\prime}_{\lambda_{j}}(\lambda_{j}))^{\alpha_{j}}}{2^{\alpha_{j}}\Gamma(\alpha_{j}+1/2)}\bigg)^{\sigma}\times

(4.93)

(\prod_{k\neq j}|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{k}|^{-\alpha_{k}\sigma})e^{-\mathcal{W}(\lambda_{j})\sigma}e^{N\lambda_{j}^{2}\sigma}=e^{N\frac{\ell}{2}\sigma}(I+R_{N}(\lambda_{j}))D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{N,\lambda_{j}}(\lambda_{j})\begin{bmatrix}0&-i\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}c_{j}^{\sigma}N^{\alpha_{j}\sigma}e^{N\lambda_{j}^{2}\sigma}.

where in the last step we have used that
f_{x}^{\prime}(x)=2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
. Combining this with (
4.86
) and observing that

(4.94)

\begin{bmatrix}0&-i\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}^{-1}=e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}e^{-\frac{\pi i}{4}\sigma}\sigma_{1}

completes the proof.
∎

4.1
Modifications for Merging Singularities

We will conclude this section by considering the modifications that need to be made when we are in the case of merging singularities required in Proposition
2.10
. In particular, for the rest of this section assume that we are in the case of
m=2
,
\mathcal{W}=0
,
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha>0
, and are allowing
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
to depend on
N
. Analysis of the Riemann-Hilbert problem with such merging singularities is undertaken in
[
9
]
, where they modify the above parametrices to obtain asymptotics in this case. We will primarily be reliant on the results on merging singularities in
[
9
,
11
]
, as well as the discussion in
[
4
]
. As we only require the supercritical case for merging singularities (i.e., we consider
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|^{-1}=O(N^{\gamma})
where
\gamma<1
), all the above results will still hold only with worse bounds on the error terms and different domains.

Away from the points
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}
though, the above asymptotics hold essentially as stated.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Then there is
\delta_{0}>0
, such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
, and any choice of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon)
(possibly
N
-dependant) with
\delta>\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}>0
, there is an error term
R_{N}
, such that for
l\geq 0
, we have that
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in both
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta\}\cup[\lambda_{2}-\delta,\lambda_{1}+\delta]
and the choice
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, and such that the following asymptotics hold: For
x\in(-1+\delta,1-\delta)\setminus(\lambda_{2}-\delta,\lambda_{1}+\delta)
, we have (
4.29
), for
|x|\geq 1+\delta
, we have (
4.30
), and for
x\in(\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta)
we have (
4.55
) or (
4.56
), for
\pm 1=1
and
\pm 1=-1
, respectively. Lastly, (
4.22
) and thus (
4.23
) hold as well, with error term uniform in the choice of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
as well.

For the remaining regions around
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
, the asymptotics of Proposition
4.13
and Proposition
4.29
occur in a smaller region and have a worse error bound.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
0<\gamma<1
. Then there is
\delta_{0}>0
, such that for
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
, and any choice of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon)
(possibly
N
-dependant) with
\delta>\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}>N^{-\gamma}
, the following holds. Denoting
r_{N}=2(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})
, there is an error term
R_{N}
, such that for
l\geq 0
, we have that
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(r_{N}^{-1})
uniformly in
x\in(\lambda_{2}-\delta,\lambda_{1}+\delta)
and the choice of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, and such that the following asymptotics hold: For
x\in(\lambda_{2}-\delta,\lambda_{2}-\delta r_{N}^{-1})\cup(\lambda_{2}+\delta r_{N}^{-1},\lambda_{1}-\delta r_{N}^{-1})\cup(\lambda_{1}+\delta r_{N}^{-1},\lambda_{1}+\delta)
, we have (
4.29
), and for
1\leq i\leq 2
, and
x\in(\lambda_{i}-\delta r_{N}^{-1},\lambda_{i}+\delta r_{N}^{-1})
, we have that (
4.71
) holds. Additionally, the asymptotics of Proposition
4.14
holds with error term now of order
O(N^{\gamma-1})
, but now uniform in the choice of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.

We will now begin to establish the prerequisites for the proofs for these statements. For the remainder of the section we will denote
u=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})^{2}/4
and
v=(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})/2
. As before, this will essentially follow from recalling and simplifying various parametrices. More specifically, in
[
9
]
they give asymptotics of
Y_{N}
in the merging case in terms of a related matrix-valued function
{}^{\prime\prime}S^{\prime\prime}
, which we denote by
S^{M}_{N}
to avoid confusion with the above
S_{N}
. To aid the reader in the translation of their notation, their
(n,t,V,a,b)
coincides with our
(N,u,-2(x-v)^{2},1-v,-1-v)
. The definition of
S^{M}_{N}
in
[
9
]
is given in (4.10) and (5.1), only employing Fig. 8 rather than Fig. 7, as explained in their Section 6.1. The construction is almost identical to the one in
[
4
]
(which we used above) with the only non-notational change being that for
x\in(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
the function
S_{N}^{M}
is defined by (
4.11
) rather than (
4.10
) (see the difference between Figure 1 of
[
4
]
and Figure 8 of
[
9
]
). In particular, the function
(S^{M}_{N})_{+}
may be given in terms of the above
(S_{N})_{+}
as follows: For
x\notin[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
,
(S^{M}_{N})_{+}(x)=(S_{N})_{+}(x)
, and for
x\in(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, we have that

(4.95)

(S_{N}^{M})_{+}(x)=(S_{N})_{+}(x)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}.

They then, as above, solve this by employing a sequence of paramatrices. We are only concerned with the case that
t>0
, which is dealt with in Section 6 of
[
9
]
. They construct paramatrices
N
,
P^{(a)}
,
P^{(b)}
, and
P
, where
P^{(a)}
is defined in a neighborhood of
1
,
P^{(b)}
is defined in a neighborhood of
-1
,
P
is defined in a neighborhood of
[\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}]
, and
N
is defined in the remainder. These first three though are defined as
P^{(\infty)}
,
P^{(1)}
, and
P^{(-1)}
in
[
4
]
, which coincide with our
P_{\infty}
,
P_{1}
, and
P_{-1}
. In particular, they prove the following result.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let us fix
\epsilon>0
. Then there is
\delta_{0}>0
such that for any
0<\delta<\delta_{0}
, and any choice of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in(-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon)
(possibly
N
-dependant) with
\delta>(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})>0
. Then there is
R_{N}
such that

(4.96)

(S^{M}_{N})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))P_{\delta}(x),

where here

(4.97)

(P_{\delta})_{+}(x)=\begin{cases}(P_{u,v})_{+}(x);\;x\in(\lambda_{2}-\delta,\lambda_{1}+\delta)\\
(P_{\pm 1})_{+}(x);\;x\in(\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta)\\
(P_{\infty})_{+}(x);\;\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

and where for any
l\geq 0
, we have that
R_{N}^{(l)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta,\lambda_{2}-\delta,\lambda_{1}+\delta\}
and the choice of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.

This result for
R_{N}
essentially follows from Section 6.5 of
[
9
]
, though they only state the error bounds for
R_{N}
. On the other hand, they actually establish that
R_{N}
satisfies a small norm Riemann-Hilbert problem, with
N
-independent contours, whose jump matrices are
O(N^{-1})
uniformly in a
N
-independent neighborhood of the contours. In particular, applying a contour deformation argument and the Cauchy integral formula as in the proof of Theorem 4.37 in
[
4
]
(see also the discussion in Section 4 of
[
30
]
), we obtain the desired error bounds on
R_{N}^{(l)}
for all
l\geq 0
. In addition, while the results of
[
9
]
establish uniformly only in
u
, the extension to uniformity in
v
is shown in Appendix G of
[
4
]
.

As the paramatrices
P_{\infty}
and
P_{\pm 1}
here coincide with those considered in the previous subsection exactly, replacing Proposition
4.2
with Proposition
4.18
as appropriate in the proofs in the previous subsection gives a proof of Proposition
4.16
(noting that
(S_{N})_{+}=(S_{N}^{M})_{+}
in all regions considered in this proposition).

On the other hand, the parametrix
P_{u,v}
does not directly coincide with any of the parametrices above, and indeed when one lets
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})=O(N^{-1})
, it appears to be quite different from a simple combination of the ones considered above. Instead, we must use the asymptotics for
P_{u,v}
in powers of
O(N^{-1}u^{-1/2})=O(N^{-1}r_{N})
which are provided in
[
11
]
, which we will show yields the following result.

Lemma 4.19
.

With the set-up of Proposition
4.18
, there is
\delta_{1}>0
, such that if
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})>\delta_{1}N^{-1}
, then there is
R_{N}(x)
such that

(4.98)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))(P_{\infty})_{+}(x);\;\;x\notin(\lambda_{2}-\sqrt{u}\delta,\lambda_{1}+\sqrt{u}\delta),

(4.99)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))(P_{\infty})_{+}(x)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix};\;\;x\in(\lambda_{2}+\sqrt{u}\delta,\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{u}\delta),

(4.100)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))(P_{\lambda_{1}})_{+}(x);\;\;\;x\in(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}+\sqrt{u}\delta),

(4.101)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))(P_{\lambda_{1}})_{+}(x)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix};\;\;x\in(\lambda_{1}-\sqrt{u}\delta,\lambda_{1}),

(4.102)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))(P_{\lambda_{2}})_{+}(x);\;\;\;x\in(\lambda_{2}-\sqrt{u}\delta,\lambda_{2}),

(4.103)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=(I+R_{N}(x))(P_{\lambda_{2}})_{+}(x)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix};\;\;x\in(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2}+\sqrt{u}\delta),

where for any
l\geq 0
, we have that
R_{N}^{(l)}(x)=O(u^{-1/2}N^{-1})
uniformly in both
x
in these regions, and in the choice of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
.

Recalling (
4.95
) for
x\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
, we see that the combined asymptotics of this lemma and Proposition
4.19
essentially coincide with those in Proposition
4.2
around
\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}
, except that now that choice of
\delta
is now
N
-dependant and the error terms are now of order
O(N^{-1}u^{-1/2})
instead of
O(N^{-1})
. Thus repeating the proofs given above, replacing
O(N^{-1})
by
O(N^{-1}u^{-1/2})=O(N^{-1}r_{N})
when necessary is sufficient to prove Proposition
4.17
.

The remainder of this subsection will consist of the proof of Lemma
4.19
. This essentially follows from Section 5 of
[
11
]
, though we will have to trace a variety of notations from
[
9
,
11
]
. A similar translation is given in Appendix G of
[
4
]
.

In preparation, let us define for
x\in\mathbb{R}

(4.104)

\lambda(x)=N(\frac{s(\lambda_{1})+s(\lambda_{2})}{2}-s(x)),\quad s_{N}=2Ni(s(\lambda_{2})-s(\lambda_{1})).

We recall a form for the parametrix
P_{u,v}
, which is defined in Section 6.4 of
[
9
]
. For
x\in(v-\delta_{0},v+\delta_{0})

(4.105)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=E_{+}(x)\Psi^{(2)}_{+}(\lambda(x);s_{N})\omega(x)^{\sigma/2}e^{Nis(x)\sigma}(\sigma\sigma_{1}),

(4.106)

E_{+}(x)=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)(\sigma\sigma_{1})^{-1}e^{i\pi\alpha\sigma}e^{-iN\frac{s(\lambda_{1})+s(\lambda_{2})}{2}\sigma},

where
\Psi^{(2)}(\lambda,s)
is a fixed matrix-valued function defined in Section 3 of
[
9
]
, which depends only on
\alpha
and
s
. We will need the asymptotics of
\Psi^{(2)}_{+}
as
s\to\infty
. To get these, we note that in view of (3.11) and (3.5) of
[
9
]
, we may write

(4.107)

\Psi^{(2)}(x,s)=\Psi_{CK}(\frac{-4x}{|s|}i;s)\begin{cases}e^{-\mathrm{Sign}(x)\pi i\alpha\sigma};\;\;|x|>|s|/4\\
I;\;\;|x|<|s|/4\end{cases},

where
\Psi_{CK}
is the a solution to the model Riemann-Hilbert problem introduced in Section 3 of
[
11
]
with parameters
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha
and
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=0
. In Section 5 of
[
11
]
uniform asymptotics for
\Psi_{CK}
in the case of
|s|\to\infty
are derived. To demonstrate these, the Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\Psi_{CK}
is solved using elementary transformations and yet another auxiliary Riemann-Hilbert problem
M
. We note for the convenience of the reader that in the case relevant to us (i.e.,
\beta=0
),
M
is expressed explicitly in terms of Hankel and modified Bessel functions in (2.47) of
[
9
]
. In particular, we see that

(4.108)

M_{+}(x)=(\sigma\sigma_{1})e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{bmatrix}(\Psi_{\alpha})_{+}(x)(\sigma\sigma_{1})^{-1}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{2}\alpha i\sigma}.

The following asymptotics of
\Psi^{(2)}(x;s)
follow from those in Section 5 of
[
11
]
and (
4.107
).

Lemma 4.20
.

There exists
s_{0}>0
and
\delta_{0}>0
such that for any
s\geq s_{0}
and
\delta_{0}>\delta>0
, there is
\bar{R}_{s}
, such that

(4.109)

\Psi^{(2)}_{+}(x;s)=(I+\bar{R}_{s}(x))e^{ix\sigma}e^{-i\alpha\pi\sigma\mathrm{Sign}(x)};\;\;|x|>\frac{|s|}{4}+|s|\delta

(4.110)

\Psi^{(2)}(x;s)=(I+\bar{R}_{s}(x))e^{ix\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix};\;\;|x|<\frac{|s|}{4}-|s|\delta

(4.111)

\Psi^{(2)}(x;s)=(I+\bar{R}_{s}(x))e^{-i\pi\alpha/2\sigma}e^{-i|s|/4\sigma}M(x)e^{\frac{3}{2}\alpha i\pi\sigma};\;\;\frac{|s|}{4}\leq x\leq\frac{|s|}{4}+|s|\delta

(4.112)

\Psi^{(2)}(x;s)=(I+\bar{R}_{s}(x))e^{-i\pi\alpha/2\sigma}e^{-i|s|/4\sigma}M(x)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha i\pi\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix};\;\;\frac{|s|}{4}-|s|\delta\leq x\leq\frac{|s|}{4}

(4.113)

\Psi^{(2)}(x;s)=(I+\bar{R}_{s}(x))e^{-\frac{3i\pi}{2}\alpha\sigma}e^{i|s|/4\sigma}M(x)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\pi\sigma};\;\;\frac{|s|}{4}\leq-x\leq\frac{|s|}{4}+|s|\delta

(4.114)

\Psi^{(2)}(x;s)=(I+\bar{R}_{s}(x))e^{-\frac{3i\pi}{2}\alpha\sigma}e^{i|s|/4\sigma}M(x)e^{\frac{3}{2}\alpha i\pi\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix};\;\;\frac{|s|}{4}-|s|\delta\leq-x\leq\frac{|s|}{4},

and such that for
l\geq 0
, we have that
\bar{R}_{s}^{(l)}(x)=O(s^{-k}(s+|x|)^{-1})
uniformly in
s\in(s_{0},\infty)
and
x
in the regions above.

Remark 4.21
.

The factor of
s^{-k}
occuring in the error term in Lemma
4.20
is due to the
s^{-1}
-scaling in (
4.107
). Indeed if one denotes by
\hat{R}_{s}
the error term for the corresponding error term in the expansion of
\Psi_{CK}(x;s)
, one has that for any
l\geq 0
,
\hat{R}_{s}^{(l)}(x)=O(s^{-1}(1+|x|)^{-1})
(see (5.25) of
[
11
]
).

Proof of Lemma
4.19
.

We recall that
\sigma_{1}\sigma=-\sigma\sigma_{1}
, so that
a^{\sigma}\sigma_{1}=\sigma a^{-\sigma}
. We also note that

(4.115)

(\sigma\sigma_{1})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}(\sigma\sigma_{1})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}.

With these results and (
4.108
), all of these expressions follow by routine computation. For example, we observe for
x\in(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1}+\sqrt{u}\delta)
that (potentially changing
\delta
)
\lambda(x)\in(|s|/4,|s|/4+\delta|s|)
so that

(4.116)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x)(\sigma\sigma_{1})^{-1}e^{i\pi\alpha\sigma}e^{-iN\frac{s(\lambda_{1})+s(\lambda_{2})}{2}\sigma}(I+\bar{R}_{s}(\lambda(x)))e^{iN\frac{s(\lambda_{1})+s(\lambda_{2})}{2}\sigma}e^{-i\alpha\pi\sigma}\omega(x)^{\sigma/2}(\sigma\sigma_{1}).

Noting that
\bar{R}_{s_{N}}(\lambda(x))=O(u^{-1/2}N^{-1})
we see that conjugating
\bar{R}_{s_{N}}
as above

(4.117)

(P_{u,v})_{+}(x)=(I+O(N^{-1}u^{-1/2}))D_{\infty}^{\sigma}T_{\infty}(x).

All claims follow from similar computations.
∎

5
Proof of Proposition
2.6

In this section, we will prove Proposition
2.6
. As
\mathcal{E}
vanishes on a neighborhood of
[-1,1]
, the integrals which compose the entries of
\Delta^{1}_{N}
are supported on regions where the integrand is exponentially small (see Remark
4.9
). Combining this with some rough estimates for the elements of
\Delta^{0}_{N}
(see Proposition
5.1
), we will be able to obtain Proposition
2.6
by employing bounds for the determinant under perturbation.

Now, as before we fix a choice of
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
1.2
. For a linear operator
A
on an inner-product space, let
\|A\|_{F}=\sqrt{\text{\rm Tr}(A^{*}A)}
denote the Frobenius norm. We recall the operators
\Delta_{N}^{1}
and
\Delta_{N}^{0}
(defined in (
2.56
) and (
2.57
)) of Section
2
, which act on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
with the inner-product
(f,g)_{w,N}=\int f(x)g(x)w_{N}(x)dx
. The main technical result of this section is the following proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

There are
C,c>0
, such that

(5.1)

\|\Delta_{N}^{1}\|_{F}=O(e^{-Nc}),\;\;\;\|\Delta_{N}^{0}\|_{F}=O(N^{C}).

Moreover for any choice of compact subset of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
, we may choose
C,c
such that these estimates are uniform on the chosen compact set.

We will now give the proof of Proposition
2.6
assuming Proposition
5.1
.

Proof of Proposition
2.6
.

We recall from Lemma
2.7
that
[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{22}=I
, so that
[\Delta_{N}]_{22}=I+[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{22}
. We observe that by Proposition
5.1
, we have that
\|[\Delta_{N}^{1}]_{22}\|_{F}=O(e^{-Nc})
. Thus by the Neumann series for the inverse, we see that
[\Delta_{N}]_{22}
is invertible and that

\|[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}-I\|_{F}=O(e^{-Nc})
. By the Schur complement formula, we have that

(5.2)

\det(\Delta_{N})=\det([\Delta_{N}]_{22})\det([\Delta_{N}]_{11}-[\Delta_{N}]_{12}[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}[\Delta_{N}]_{21}).

Now recall that
[\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{12}=0
so that
[\Delta_{N}]_{12}=[\Delta_{N}^{1}]_{12}
. Thus we have that

(5.3)

\|[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{11}-[\Delta_{N}]_{12}[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}[\Delta_{N}]_{21}\|_{F}=

(5.4)

\|[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{11}-[\Delta_{N}^{1}]_{12}[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}[\Delta_{N}]_{21}\|_{F}\leq\|[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{11}\|_{F}+\|[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{12}\|_{F}\|[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}\|_{F}\|[\Delta_{N}]_{21}\|_{F}.

The work above shows that
\|[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}\|_{F}=\|I\|_{F}+O(e^{-Nc})=O(N)
, and by Proposition
5.1
we have that
\|[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{11}\|_{F},\|[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{12}\|_{F}=O(e^{-Nc})
. Combining this with the above inequality, we see that

(5.5)

\|[\Delta^{1}_{N}]_{11}-[\Delta_{N}]_{12}[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}[\Delta_{N}]_{21}\|_{F}=O(e^{-Nc/2}).

We recall the bound on
\ell
-by-
\ell
matrices
A
and
B
(see Theorem 2.12 of
[
25
]
)

(5.6)

|\det(A)-\det(B)|\leq\ell\|A-B\|_{F}(\max(\|A\|_{F},\|B\|_{F}))^{\ell-1}.

Recalling that
\dim(\mathcal{P}_{N,1})=m+d
, we see that by applying this inequality, the bound of (
5.5
), and Proposition
5.1

(5.7)

|\det([\Delta_{N}]_{11}-[\Delta_{N}]_{12}[\Delta_{N}]_{22}^{-1}[\Delta_{N}]_{21})-\det([\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{11})|=O(e^{-Nc/4}).

We will further need the bound on
\ell
-by-
\ell
matrices
A
(see Corollary 2.14 of
[
25
]
)

(5.8)

|\det(I+A)-1|\leq(\ell\|A\|_{F}+1)^{\ell}-1.

Employing the formula
[\Delta_{N}]_{22}=I+[\Delta_{N}^{1}]_{22}
, as well as Proposition
5.1
, this bound yields that
|\det([\Delta_{N}]_{22})-1|=O(e^{-Nc/2})
.
Using this, (
5.2
), and (
5.7
), we have that

(5.9)

\det(\Delta_{N})=\det([\Delta_{N}]_{22})(\det([\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{11})+O(e^{-Nc/4}))=\det([\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{11})(1+O(e^{-Nc/2}))+O(e^{-Nc/4}).

Finally applying Proposition
5.1
to see that
\det([\Delta_{N}^{0}]_{11})=O(e^{Nc/4})
, and recalling that
\det([\Delta^{0}_{N}]_{11})=\det(\Delta^{0}_{N})
completes the proof.
∎

The remainder of the section will be spent on the proof of Proposition
5.1
. We first note that by Proposition
2.5
we have that

(5.10)

\|\Delta^{1}_{N}\|_{F}=\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}^{\perp}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}\leq\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}+\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|_{F},

(5.11)

\|\Delta^{0}_{N}\|_{F}=\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}J_{N}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}\leq\|I\|_{F}+2N\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}x\Pi_{N}\|_{F}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}|\alpha_{i}|\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{i}q_{i}^{T}\Pi_{N}\|_{F},

where here, the transpose
f^{T}
is taken with respect to the inner-product on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
inherited from
L^{2}(w_{N})
. Thus it suffices to compute the order of all terms on the right. The terms
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}
,
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}
and
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}x\Pi_{N}\|_{F}
will all follow from the same method, which we now illustrate. Let
A
be an operator on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
with Schwartz kernel
Af(x)=\int G(x,y)f(y)w_{N}(y)dy
. Then evaluating the Frobenius norm with respect to the basis given by orthogonal polynomials, we see that

(5.12)

\|A\|^{2}_{F}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\int\int\int G(x,y)G(x,z)p_{i}(y)p_{i}(z)w_{N}(x)w_{N}(y)w_{N}(z)dxdydz=

(5.13)

\int\int\int G(x,y)G(x,z)K_{N}(y,z)w_{N}(x)w_{N}(y)w_{N}(z)dxdydz.

Let us denote
\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,y)=w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}K_{N}(x,y)
where
K_{N}
is the Christoffel-Darboux kernel. In the sense above, the operator
\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}
has kernel
G(x,y)=w_{N}^{-1/2}(x)(\frac{1}{2}\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,z)\mathrm{Sign}(z-y)dz)\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(y)w_{N}^{-1/2}(y)
on
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. We may employ (
5.13
) to obtain

(5.14)

\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|^{2}_{F}=

(5.15)

\frac{1}{4}\int\cdots\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(x_{1},x_{2})\mathrm{Sign}(x_{2}-x_{3})\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(x_{3})\mathcal{K}_{N}(x_{1},x_{4})\mathrm{Sign}(x_{4}-x_{5})\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(x_{5})\mathcal{K}_{N}(x_{3},x_{5})\prod_{i=1}^{5}dx_{i}.

By taking the absolute value of the integrand, we may upper-bound this quantity by

(5.16)

\frac{1}{4}(\int\int\int|\mathcal{K}_{N}(x_{1},x_{2})\mathcal{K}_{N}(x_{1},x_{4})|dx_{1}dx_{2}dx_{4})(\int\int|\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(x_{3})\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(x_{5})\mathcal{K}_{N}(x_{3},x_{5})|dx_{3}dx_{5}).

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we have that
|\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,y)|\leq\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,x)^{1/2}\mathcal{K}_{N}(y,y)^{1/2}
. Repeatedly applying this bound to (
5.16
), and observing that
\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,x)dx=N
, we see that
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|^{2}_{F}\leq\frac{N}{4}I_{1}^{2}I_{2}^{2}
,
where

(5.17)

I_{1}=\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(w,w)^{1/2}dw,\;\;I_{2}=\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(w,w)^{1/2}|\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(w)|dw.

To compute these integrals let us denote
\varphi_{N,1}(x)=p_{N}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}
and
\varphi_{N,2}(x)=p_{N-1}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}
.
We note that by Remark
4.9
for any
\delta>0
there is
c>0
small enough so that for
|x|>1+\delta

(5.18)

\frac{d}{dx}\varphi_{N,i}(x),\varphi_{N,i}(x)=O(\exp(-cNx^{2})).

By the Christoffel-Darboux formula (
2.40
) and Lemma
4.5
, we see that
\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,x)=O(\exp(-cNx^{2}))
uniformly on
|x|>1+\delta
. Now applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we see that

(5.19)

I_{1}=\int_{|w|>1+\delta}\mathcal{K}_{N}(w,w)^{1/2}dw+\int_{|w|<1+\delta}\mathcal{K}_{N}(w,w)^{1/2}dw\leq

(5.20)

\int_{|w|>1+\delta}\mathcal{K}_{N}(w,w)^{1/2}dw+(2+2\delta)^{1/2}(\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(w,w)dw)^{1/2}.

The integral on the right-hand side of (
5.20
) is seen to be of order
O(e^{-Nc/4})
using the above asymptotic, and the second is exactly
(2+2\delta)^{1/2}N^{1/2}
, so that
I_{1}=O(N^{1/2})
. When applying
the same trick to
I_{2}
, the first term is
O(e^{-Nc/4})
, and the latter vanishes, so that
I_{2}=O(e^{-Nc/4})
, so that
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}^{2}=O(e^{-Nc/8})
. The same method works to show that
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}=O(e^{-Nc/4})
and that
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\Pi_{N}x\Pi_{N}\|_{F}=O(N^{5})
.

We now focus our attention to
\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{i}q_{i}^{T}\Pi_{N}\|_{F}
. In this case we see that, recalling (
2.55
) and applying again the inequality
|\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,y)|\leq\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,x)^{1/2}\mathcal{K}_{N}(y,y)^{1/2}
,

(5.21)

\|\Pi_{N}J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{i}q_{i}^{T}\Pi_{N}\|^{2}_{F}=

(5.22)

e^{-2N\lambda_{i}^{2}}N^{-2\alpha_{i}}K_{N}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i})\int\int K_{N}(x_{1},x_{2})J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{i}(x_{1})J_{N}^{-1}\ell_{i}(x_{2})w_{N}(x_{1})w_{N}(x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}\leq

(5.23)

e^{-2N\lambda_{i}^{2}}N^{-2\alpha_{i}}K_{N}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i})(\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,x)^{1/2}|\epsilon(\ell_{i}w_{N}^{1/2})(x)|dx)^{2},

where here

(5.24)

\epsilon(f)(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)f(y)dy.

To understand these quantities we will first employ Proposition
4.71
to write, for
x\in(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)
and
k=1,2

(5.25)

\varphi_{N,k}(x)=G_{N,k}(x)\mathcal{J}_{\alpha_{i}}(Nf_{\lambda_{i}}(x)),\;\;G_{N,k}(x)=i^{I(k=2)}\pi^{-1/2}e_{k}^{T}(I+R_{N}(x))T_{N,\lambda_{i}}(x).

We also recall some classical asymptotics of Bessel functions (see Chapter 9 of
[
1
]
): for
\nu>-1
and
x\in(0,\infty)
, we have that

(5.26)

x^{-\nu}J_{\nu}(x),\;\frac{d}{dx}(x^{-\nu}J_{\nu}(x))=O((1+x)^{-\nu-1/2}),

where both the errors are uniform in
x\in(0,\infty)
. From this, we see that for
\nu>-1/2
and for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.27)

x^{-\nu}\mathcal{J}_{\nu}(x),\;\frac{d}{dx}(x^{-\nu}\mathcal{J}_{\nu}(x))=O((1+|x|)^{-\nu+1/2}),

where again, the error term is uniform in
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We will denote
\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(x)=\varphi_{N,k}(x)|x-\lambda_{i}|^{-\alpha_{i}}
, which removes the singular part of
\varphi_{N,k}
around
\lambda_{i}
coming from
w_{N}(x)^{1/2}
. Now employing (
5.27
) and Remark
4.12
to the representation in (
5.25
), we see that for
x\in(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)
and
k=1,2
, we have that,

(5.28)

\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(x),\;\frac{d}{dx}\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(x)=O(N^{\alpha_{i}+2}),

again uniformly in
x
and compact subsets of
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
.
Applying this to the Christoffel-Darboux formula (
2.40
), we obtain that

(5.29)

e^{-2N\lambda_{i}^{2}}N^{-2\alpha_{i}}K_{N}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i})=O(N^{8}).

We now focus on the term
\epsilon(\ell_{i}w_{N}^{1/2})(x)
. We note that

(5.30)

\left|\int\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\frac{\varphi_{N,k}(y)dy}{y-\lambda_{i}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{|y-\lambda_{i}|>\delta}|\varphi_{N,k}(y)|dy+\left|\int_{|y-\lambda_{i}|<\delta}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\frac{\varphi_{N,k}(y)}{y-\lambda_{i}}dy\right|.

Using that
\int\varphi_{N,k}^{2}(y)dy=1
and proceeding as in (
5.20
), we may see that the first integral on the right-hand side of (
5.30
) is
O(1)
, leading us to bound the second. We have that

(5.31)

\int_{|y-\lambda_{i}|<\delta}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\frac{\varphi_{N,k}(y)}{y-\lambda_{i}}dy=\int_{|y-\lambda_{i}|<\delta}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\frac{(\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(y)-\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(\lambda_{i}))|y-\lambda_{i}|^{\alpha_{i}}}{y-\lambda_{i}}dy+

(5.32)

\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(\lambda_{i})\int_{|y-\lambda_{i}|<\delta}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\frac{|y-\lambda_{i}|^{\alpha_{i}}}{y-\lambda_{i}}dy.

The latter term is
O(N^{\alpha_{i}+2})
by (
5.28
). Additionally, applying again (
5.28
) and the mean value theorem to
\varphi_{N,k,i}
, we see that

(5.33)

\int_{|y-\lambda_{i}|<\delta}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)\frac{(\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(y)-\hat{\varphi}_{N,k}(\lambda_{i}))|y-\lambda_{i}|^{\alpha_{i}}}{y-\lambda_{i}}dy=O(N^{\alpha_{i}+2}).

These bounds are additionally uniform in
y
, so that in particular we may conclude that for
k=1,2
that
\epsilon(\varphi_{N,k})(x)=O(N^{\alpha_{i}+2})
, uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}
. By Remark
4.15
, we see that
\epsilon(\ell_{i}w_{N}^{1/2})(x)=O(N^{\alpha_{i}+2})
.
Again proceeding as in (
5.20
), we obtain that

(5.34)

\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,x)^{1/2}\epsilon(\ell_{i}w_{N}^{1/2})(x)dx=O(N^{\alpha_{i}+4}).

In conclusion, we obtain that

(5.35)

e^{-2N\lambda_{i}^{2}}K_{N}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{i})(\int\mathcal{K}_{N}(x,x)^{1/2}\epsilon(\ell_{i}w_{N}^{1/2})(x)dx)^{2}=O(N^{16(1+\alpha_{i})}).

In total these results show that the right-hand side of (
5.10
) is of order
O(e^{-Nc/4})
, and that the right-hand side of (
5.11
) is of order
O(N^{16(1+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i})})
, which together completes the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

6
Proof of Proposition
2.9

In this section, we will prove Proposition
2.9
. The evaluation of these integrals is a key technical step in this paper. Away from the points
\{\pm 1\}
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
this computation will follow by standard methods for oscillatory integrals (see for example Lemma
6.3
). The remainder of the work is then spent understanding the contributions around the points
\{\pm 1\}
and
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
. This will follow from applications of the classical asymptotics for the Bessel and Airy functions and careful analysis to compare these integrals with those in the remainder of the bulk. There is some similarity with methods used in
[
13
,
12
,
14
]
, particularly for the asymptotics away from the points
\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
.

To begin, we first note that we may write

(6.1)

\int J_{N}^{-1}f(x)g(x)w_{N}(x)dx=\frac{1}{2}\int\int\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)g(x)f(y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy.

We observe that if functions
f
and
g
are supported on disjoint intervals, then we have that

(6.2)

\int\int\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)g(x)f(y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=\pm\int g(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx\int f(y)w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dy.

This will be an important observation. With this identity and a partition of unity, we will reduce the integrals into a sequence of simpler integrals supported over regions where a single asymptotic expression holds. We again fix
(\mathcal{W},\{\lambda_{i}\}_{i},\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i})
satisfying the conditions of Proposition
1.2
, and in all results below, the error will be uniform in a neighborhood of
\{\lambda_{i}\}\in\{(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{m})\in(-1,1)^{m}:\lambda_{1}>\cdots>\lambda_{m}\}
. We will employ, as before, the notation
\lambda_{0}=1
and
\lambda_{m+1}=-1
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Assume that
\phi
is a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
of sub-exponential growth. Assume that there is some
\delta>0
such that
\phi
vanishes on
(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)
for all
0\leq i\leq m+1
. Then for each
k
and any
c\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

(6.3)

\int\phi(x)p_{N-k}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=O(N^{-c}).

Proof.

For notational clarity, we will assume that
k=0
. The proof will proceed by applying the results of Section
4
on each region. We first note that

(6.4)

\int\phi(x)p_{N}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=\int_{|x|>1+\delta}\phi(x)p_{N}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx+\int_{|x|<1}\phi(x)p_{N}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx.

We note that by Remark
4.9
we have that

(6.5)

\int_{|x|>1+\delta}\phi(x)p_{N}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=O(e^{-NC/2}).

We now focus on the computation of
\int_{|x|<1}\phi(x)p_{N}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx
. If
\phi
is supported on
(\lambda_{i+1}+\delta,\lambda_{i}-\delta)
, for some
0\leq i\leq m
, we may apply Proposition
4.7
to rewrite

(6.6)

\int_{|x|<1}\phi(x)p_{N}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=\frac{1}{\pi^{1/2}}\bigg[\int\phi(x)[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)]_{11}e^{-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}}e^{iNs(x)}dx+

(6.7)

\int\phi(x)[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)]_{12}e^{i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}}e^{-iNs(x)}dx\bigg],

where
R_{N}
is the error term of Proposition
4.7
. We now recall that if
f
is a smooth function of compact support, then for any
c\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that

(6.8)

|\int e^{-iNx}f(x)dx|\leq\frac{1}{N^{c}}\int|f^{(c)}(x)|dx.

This inequality, and a change of variables, will be sufficient to complete the proof. Indeed, let us consider the first term of (
6.7
) and denote
f_{N,l}(x)=\phi(x)[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)]_{1l}e^{(-1)^{l}i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{i}\alpha_{k}}
. As we have that for any
l\geq 0
,
R^{(l)}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly on the support of
\phi
, and recalling that by Remark
4.8
,
T_{\infty}(x)
is
N
-independent and smooth, we see that for any
c\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
f_{N,l}^{(c)}(x)=O(1)
uniformly for
x\in\mathbb{R}
. By (
6.8
) we see that

(6.9)

|\int f_{N,l}(x)e^{\pm iNs(x)}dx|=|\int(s^{-1})^{\prime}(y)f_{N,l}(s^{-1}(y))e^{\pm iNy}dy|\leq\frac{1}{N^{c}}\int|\frac{d^{c}}{dy^{c}}\left((s^{-1})^{\prime}(y)f_{N,l}(s^{-1}(y))\right)|dy.

As
s
is smooth and non-degenerate on the support of
f_{N}
, we see that the integrand of the right-hand side is uniformly
O(1)
, and supported inside of the compact set
[s(1),s(-1)]=[0,\pi]
, so that terms of (
6.7
) are of order
O(N^{-c})
. Applying this argument to each region completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\phi
and
\varphi
be smooth functions of subexponential growth on
\mathbb{R}
. Assume that there is some
\delta>0
such that both
\phi
and
\varphi
vanishes on
(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)
for all
0\leq i\leq m+1
.
Then for each
n
and
m
, we have that

(6.10)

\frac{1}{2}\int\int\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=

(6.11)

\frac{1}{2\pi N}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}\sin((m-n)\mathrm{arccos}(x))dx+O(N^{-2}).

Proof.

Applying a partition of unity and linearity, we may assume we are in the case where
\phi
is supported on
(\lambda_{i+1}+\delta,\lambda_{i}-\delta)
for some
i
, or on
(1+\delta,\infty)\cup(-\infty,-1-\delta)
, and similarly for
\psi
. In the case where
\psi
and
\phi
have disjoint support, we see by (
6.2
) that

(6.12)

|\int\int\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy|=

(6.13)

|\int\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx\int\varphi(y)p_{N-m}(y)w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dy|.

By Lemma
6.1
, both of these integrals on the right-hand side are
O(N^{-2})
. Thus we may assume that we are in the case where either both
\psi
and
\phi
are supported on
(\lambda_{i+1}+\delta,\lambda_{i}-\delta)
for some
i
, or on
(1+\delta,\infty)\cup(-\infty,-1-\delta)
.
In the latter case, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have that

(6.14)

|\int\int_{\min(|x|,|y|)>1+\delta}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy|\leq

(6.15)

(\int_{|x|>1+\delta}p_{N-n}(x)^{2}\phi(x)^{2}w_{N}(x)dx\int_{|y|>1+\delta}p_{N-m}(y)^{2}\varphi(y)^{2}w_{N}(y)dy)^{1/2}

Then applying Remark
4.9
as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we see that the right-hand side of (
6.15
) is
O(e^{-NC/4})
.

Thus we may assume that
\phi
and
\varphi
are both supported on
(\lambda_{i+1}+\delta,\lambda_{i}-\delta)
for some
i
. To compute this integral, we will use the following lemma (see Chapter 8.4 of
[
5
]
).

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
h
be a smooth, compactly supported function on
\mathbb{R}
. Then we have that

(6.16)

\frac{1}{2}\int\int h(x,y)e^{\pm iN(x+y)}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy=O(N^{-2})

(6.17)

\frac{1}{2}\int\int h(x,y)e^{\pm iN(x-y)}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy=\pm\frac{i}{N}\int h(x,x)dx+O(N^{-2}).

More specifically, the error may be bounded by

\frac{1}{N^{2}}(\int\int\|\nabla h(x,y)\|dxdy+4\int|\frac{d}{dx}h(x,x)|dx)
.

Using the asymptotics of Proposition
4.29
, we may write

(6.18)

\frac{1}{2}\int\int\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=

(6.19)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j,k=1,2}\int\int f_{N,j,k}(x,y)e^{-(-1)^{j}Nis(x)-(-1)^{k}Nis(y)}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy,

where here

(6.20)

f_{N,j,k}(x,y)=\phi(x)\varphi(y)[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{\infty}(x)]_{1j}[(I+\bar{R}_{N}(y))T_{\infty}(y)]_{1k}\times

(6.21)

\exp((-1)^{j}i(n\mathrm{arccos}(x)+\pi\sum_{l=1}^{i}\alpha_{l})+(-1)^{k}i(m\mathrm{arccos}(y)+\pi\sum_{l=1}^{i}\alpha_{l})),

and
R_{N}
and
\bar{R}_{N}
denote the error terms in Proposition
4.7
for the case of
n
and
m
, respectively. Recalling Remark
4.8
as before, as well as the bound
R^{(c)}_{N}(x),\bar{R}^{(c)}_{N}(y)=O(N^{-1})
for any
c\in\mathbb{N}
, we see that for
j,k\in\{1,2\}
, we have that
f_{N,j,k}(x,y)=O(1)
and
\|\nabla f_{N,j,k}(x,y)\|=O(1)
, both uniformly in
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.
From this, we see that

(6.22)

\|\nabla((s^{-1})^{\prime}(x)(s^{-1})^{\prime}(y)f_{N,j,k}(s^{-1}(x),s^{-1}(y)))\|=O(1)

uniformly in
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus applying Lemma
6.3
and a change of variables with respect to
s
, and recalling that
s^{\prime}(x)<0
for
x\in(-1,1)
, so that
\mathrm{Sign}(s(x)-s(y))=-\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)
, we see that

(6.23)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j,k=1,2}\int\int f_{N,j,k}(x,y)e^{-(-1)^{j}Nis(x)-(-1)^{k}Nis(y)}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy=

(6.24)

-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j,k=1,2}\int\int|(s^{-1})^{\prime}(x)(s^{-1})^{\prime}(y)|f_{N,j,k}(s^{-1}(x),s^{-1}(y))e^{-(-1)^{j}Nix-(-1)^{k}Niy}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy=

(6.25)

-\frac{i}{\pi N}\int\frac{f_{N,1,2}(x,x)}{|s^{\prime}(x)|}dx+\frac{i}{\pi N}\int\frac{f_{N,2,1}(x,x)}{|s^{\prime}(x)|}dx+O(N^{-2}).

We now observe that as
R_{N}(x),\bar{R}_{N}(x)=O(N^{-1})
, we have that

(6.26)

f_{N,j,k}(x,y)=\phi(x)\varphi(y)[T_{\infty}(x)]_{1j}[T_{\infty}(y)]_{1k}e^{(-1)^{j}i(n\mathrm{arccos}(x)+\pi\sum_{l=1}^{i}\alpha_{l})+(-1)^{k}i(m\mathrm{arccos}(y)+\pi\sum_{l=1}^{i}\alpha_{l})}+O(N^{-1}).

We may express the right-hand side of (
6.25
) as

(6.27)

\frac{i}{\pi N}\int\int\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{|s^{\prime}(x)|}[T_{\infty}(x)]_{11}[T_{\infty}(x)]_{12}(e^{i(n-m)\mathrm{arccos}(x)}-e^{-i(n-m)\mathrm{arccos}(x)})dx+O(N^{-2}).

Combining this with the identities
[T_{\infty}(x)]_{11}[T_{\infty}(x)]_{12}=\frac{1}{2(1-x^{2})^{1/2}}
and
s^{\prime}(x)=-2\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
, we obtain that

(6.28)

\frac{i}{4\pi N}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}(e^{i(n-m)\mathrm{arccos}(x)}-e^{-i(n-m)\mathrm{arccos}(x)})dx+O(N^{-2})=

(6.29)

\frac{1}{2\pi N}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}\sin((m-n)\mathrm{arccos}(x))dx+O(N^{-2}).

Considered altogether, these results yield the desired claim.
∎

We will now begin to compute integrals of functions supported around
\lambda_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
. It will be convenient to prove a result to match the behavior of the asymptotics of Proposition
4.7
and
4.13
over their overlapping regions.

We recall the asymptotics for the Bessel function (see Chapter 9 of
[
1
]
):

(6.30)

J_{\nu}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi x}}\bigg[\cos(x-\frac{\nu\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{4})+O(x^{-1})\bigg].

By employing this expansion, we obtain that with
\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}
defined as in (
4.70
), we have that,

(6.31)

\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(x)=(I+O(x^{-1}))\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(x),\quad\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{Sign}(x)\sqrt{2}\sin(|x|-\frac{\alpha\pi}{2})\\
\sqrt{2}\cos(|x|-\frac{\alpha\pi}{2})\end{bmatrix},

uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}
. We will make use of the following matching computation.

Lemma 6.4
.

For any
q\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in(-1,1)
, we have that

(6.32)

T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}(x)\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{j}+q}((N-k)(s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})-s_{N,k}(x)))=

(6.33)

T_{\infty}(x)e^{(-i\pi\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{k}-i\pi\alpha_{j}I(x<\lambda_{j}))\sigma}e^{i\mathrm{Sign}(x-\lambda_{j})\frac{\pi}{2}q\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)}\\
e^{-i(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)}\end{bmatrix}.

Proof.

We observe that as
s_{N,k}
is monotonically decreasing, if we denote
\pm=\mathrm{Sign}(x-\lambda_{j})=\mathrm{Sign}(s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})-s_{N,k}(x))
then

(6.34)

\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{j}+q}((N-k)(s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})-s_{N,k}(x)))=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{2}\sin((N-k)(s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})-s_{N,k}(x))\mp(\alpha_{j}+q)\frac{\pi}{2})\\
\sqrt{2}\cos((N-k)(s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})-s_{N,k}(x))\mp(\alpha_{j}+q)\frac{\pi}{2})\end{bmatrix}.

We also note that for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that

(6.35)

e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{2}\sin(b-a)\\
\sqrt{2}\cos(b-a)\end{bmatrix}=e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}ie^{i(a-b)}\\
e^{-i(a-b)}\end{bmatrix}=e^{ia\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{-ib}\\
e^{ib}\end{bmatrix},

so that we see that

(6.36)

e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
i&1\end{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{j}+q}((N-k)(s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})-s_{N,k}(x)))=e^{\pm\frac{i\pi}{2}(\alpha_{j}+q)\sigma}e^{-i(N-k)s_{N,k}(\lambda_{j})\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)}\\
e^{-i(N-k)s_{N,k}(x)}\end{bmatrix}.

Multiplying both sides of (
6.36
) by
T_{\infty}(x)e^{i\frac{\rho_{N,k,j}}{2}\sigma}
, and recalling the definitions of
T_{N,k,\lambda_{j}}
given in (
4.67
), we see that the left-hand and right-hand sides simplify to the left-hand and right-hand sides of (
6.33
).
∎

We will also need the following elementary result, which will follow by integration by parts.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
f,g:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be monotone increasing smooth bijections, with everywhere positive derivatives on
(0,\infty)
. Let
h,l
be smooth functions of compact support and let
F,G
be continuous functions, all defined on
[0,\infty)
. Then we have that

(6.37)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}h(x)l(y)F(f(x))G(g(y))\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy=I+II+III,

where here

(6.38)

I=\int_{0}^{\infty}F(f(x))(\int_{0}^{g(x)}G(z)dz)\frac{h(x)l(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)}dx,

(6.39)

II=\int_{0}^{\infty}(\int_{0}^{f(x)}F(z)dz)(\int_{0}^{g(x)}G(z)dz)\frac{h(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)}\frac{d}{dx}(\frac{l(x)}{g^{\prime}(x)})dx,

(6.40)

III=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\int_{0}^{f(x)}F(z)dz)(\int_{0}^{g(y)}G(z)dz)\frac{d}{dx}(\frac{h(x)}{f^{\prime}(x)})\frac{d}{dy}(\frac{l(y)}{g^{\prime}(y)})\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy.

Proof.

We first introduce the functions
\bar{h}(x)=h(f^{-1}(x))(f^{-1})^{\prime}(x)
and
\bar{l}(x)=l(g^{-1}(x))(g^{-1})^{\prime}(x)
, so that we may write the left-hand side of (
6.37
) as

(6.41)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{h}(u)\bar{l}(v)F(u)G(v)\mathrm{Sign}(f^{-1}(u)-g^{-1}(v))dudv.

We observe that by monotonicity of
g
we have that
\mathrm{Sign}(f^{-1}(u)-g^{-1}(v))=\mathrm{Sign}(g(f^{-1}(u))-v)
, and furthermore by integration by parts we see that for
u>0

(6.42)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{l}(v)G(v)\mathrm{Sign}(g(f^{-1}(u))-v)dv=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{g(f^{-1}(u))}\bar{l}(v)G(v)dv-\frac{1}{2}\int_{g(f^{-1}(u))}^{\infty}\bar{l}(v)G(v)dv=

(6.43)

\bar{l}(g(f^{-1}(u)))(\int_{0}^{g(f^{-1}(u))}G(z)dz)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{l}^{\prime}(v)(\int_{0}^{v}G(z)dz)\mathrm{Sign}(g(f^{-1}(u))-v)dv.

Applying this, we see that (
6.41
) may be rewritten as the sum
I^{\prime}+II^{\prime}
where

(6.44)

I^{\prime}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{h}(u)F(u)\bar{l}(g(f^{-1}(u)))(\int_{0}^{g(f^{-1}(u))}G(z)dz)du,

(6.45)

II^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bar{h}(u)F(u)\bar{l}^{\prime}(v)(\int_{0}^{v}G(z)dz)\mathrm{Sign}(g(f^{-1}(u))-v)dudv.

Again applying a change of variables with respect to
f
, and observing that
\bar{l}(g(y))=l(y)/g^{\prime}(y)
, we see that
I=I^{\prime}
. Similarly, noting that

(6.46)

\bar{l}^{\prime}(g(y))=\frac{l^{\prime}(y)}{(g^{\prime}(y))^{2}}-\frac{l(y)g^{\prime\prime}(y)}{(g^{\prime}(y))^{3}}=\frac{1}{g^{\prime}(y)}\frac{d}{dy}\left(\frac{l(y)}{g^{\prime}(y)}\right)

we see that

(6.47)

II^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}F(f(x))(\int_{0}^{g(y)}G(z)dz)h(x)\frac{d}{dy}(\frac{l(y)}{g^{\prime}(y)})\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy.

By applying essentially the same argument, integrating by parts with respect to
x
instead of
y
, we may show that
II^{\prime}=II+III
. More specifically, we may observe that if we swap the dummy-variables
(x,y)
in
II^{\prime}
, then
II^{\prime}
is just the left-hand side of (
6.37
) with a modified set of functions, so repeating the above argument, one may indeed confirm that
II^{\prime}=II+III
.
∎

We are now able to state the appropriate generalizations of the above lemmas to the case of functions supported around
\lambda_{i}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

For each
1\leq i\leq m
, there exists
\delta>0
, such that if
\phi
and
\varphi
are smooth
functions supported on
(\lambda_{i}-\delta,\lambda_{i}+\delta)
, then we have for any
n
and
m
that

(6.48)

\int\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=O(N^{-1}),

(6.49)

\frac{1}{2}\int\int\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=

(6.50)

\frac{1}{2\pi N}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}\sin((m-n)\mathrm{arccos}(x))dx+O(N^{-2}).

Proof.

For convenience, let us denote
\lambda=\lambda_{i}
and
\alpha=\alpha_{i}
for this proof. We choose
\delta>0
so the asymptotics of Proposition
4.13
hold on
(\lambda-2\delta,\lambda+2\delta)
. We recall the following lemma due to
[
34
]
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
h
be a smooth, compactly supported function, and assume that
\mu+\nu>-1
. Then we have that

(6.51)

\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{\nu}(Nt)t^{\mu}h(t)dt-D(\nu,\mu)\frac{h(0)}{N^{\mu+1}}=O(N^{-\mu-2}),\;\;\;D(\nu,\mu)=2^{\mu}\frac{\Gamma((\mu+\nu+1)/2)}{\Gamma((\nu-\mu+1)/2)}.

More specifically, the error is bounded by:

\frac{C_{\mu,\nu}}{N^{\mu+2}}(\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil\mu+3/2\rceil}\int_{0}^{\infty}|h^{(i)}(x)|dx)

for some absolute constant
C_{\mu,\nu}>0
.

From Proposition
4.13
, we may write

(6.52)

\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}=h_{N,1}(x)\mathrm{Sign}(x-\lambda)\sqrt{\pi|(N-n)f_{N,n,\lambda}(x)|}J_{\alpha+1/2}((N-n)f_{N,n,\lambda}(x))+

(6.53)

h_{N,2}(x)\sqrt{\pi|(N-n)f_{N,n,\lambda}(x)|}J_{\alpha-1/2}((N-n)f_{N,n,\lambda}(x)),

where here
h_{N,k}(x)=\phi(x)\frac{1}{\pi^{1/2}}[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{N,n,\lambda}(x)]_{1k}
. We recall from Remark
4.12
that for
c\in\mathbb{N}
and
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, we have that
[T_{N,n,\lambda}^{(c)}(x)]_{ij}=O(1)
uniformly in
x\in(\lambda-\delta,\lambda+\delta)
. As we also have that
R^{(c)}(x)=O(N^{-1})
uniformly, we see that
h_{N,k}^{(c)}(x)=O(1)
for
c\in\mathbb{N}
. From this, we see from Lemma
6.7
and a change of coordinates that there is
C
, only dependant on
\alpha
, such that

(6.54)

|\int_{\lambda}^{\lambda\pm\delta}\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx|\leq N^{-1}\pi^{1/2}(D(\alpha+1/2,1/2)|h_{N,1}(\lambda)|+

(6.55)

D(\alpha-1/2,1/2)|h_{N,2}(\lambda)|)|(f_{N,n,\lambda}^{-1})^{\prime}(0)|+\frac{C}{N^{2}}\sum_{k=1,2}\sum_{l=0}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\frac{d^{l}}{dx^{l}}\left((f_{N,n,\lambda}^{-1})^{\prime}(x)h_{N,k}(f_{N,n,\lambda}^{-1}(x))\right)|dx.

We see as above that the integrands on the right-hand side of (
6.55
) are
O(1)
, so we see that

(6.56)

\int_{\lambda}^{\lambda\pm\delta}\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=O(N^{-1}),

which establishes the first claim.

The case of double integrals will require more care. We note that by (
6.2
) and (
6.56
), we have that

(6.57)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\lambda}^{\lambda\pm\delta}\int_{\lambda}^{\lambda\mp\delta}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=O(N^{-2}),

as we may split this double integral into a product of the single variable integrals dealt with above. Thus by breaking the double-integral into four regions, we see that

(6.58)

\frac{1}{2}\int\int\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=

(6.59)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy+

(6.60)

\frac{1}{2}\int^{\lambda}_{-\infty}\int^{\lambda}_{-\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy+O(N^{-2}).

We compute the first integral, with the other case being identical. For notational ease, we will denote
g_{N,k}(x)=(N-k)f_{N,k,\lambda}(x)
. In view of Proposition
4.13
, we further denote
h_{N,i}(x)=\phi(x)[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{N,n,\lambda}(x)]_{1i}
and
l_{N,i}(x)=\varphi(x)[(I+\bar{R}_{N}(x))T_{N,m,\lambda}(x)]_{1i}
, where
R_{N}
and
\bar{R}_{N}
denote the error terms in Proposition
4.13
for case of
k=n
and
k=m
, respectively. Thus by employing Proposition
4.13
, we may rewrite the first integral on the right-hand side of (
6.60
) as

(6.61)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}h_{N,k}(x)[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{k}l_{N,l}(y)[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(g_{N,m}(y))]_{l}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy.

We now observe that
f_{N,k,\lambda}^{\prime}(x)=2\eta^{-1}_{k}\sqrt{1-\eta_{k}^{-2}x^{2}}
, and that
f_{N,k,\lambda}(\lambda)=0
. Thus by Lemma
6.5
we see that for
k
fixed and
N
sufficiently large, we may rewrite (
6.61
) as
I+II+III
where

(6.62)

I=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{k}(\int_{\lambda}^{g_{N,m}(x)}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(z)]_{l}dz)\frac{h_{N,k}(x)l_{N,l}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)}dx,

(6.63)

II=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}(\int_{\lambda}^{g_{N,n}(x)}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(z)]_{k}dz)(\int_{\lambda}^{g_{N,m}(x)}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(z)]_{l}dz)\frac{h_{N,k}(x)}{g_{N,n}^{\prime}(x)}\frac{d}{dx}(\frac{l_{N,l}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)})dx,

(6.64)

III=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}(\int_{\lambda}^{g_{N,n}(x)}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(z)]_{k}dz)(\int_{\lambda}^{g_{N,m}(y)}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(z)]_{l}dz)\times

(6.65)

(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{h_{N,k}(x)}{g_{N,n}^{\prime}(x)})(\frac{d}{dy}\frac{l_{N,l}(y)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(y)})\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy.

We now recall another important asymptotic for understanding these terms. Assuming that
\ell+\nu>-1
, we may define

(6.66)

J_{\nu,\ell}(x)=\int_{0}^{x}y^{\ell}J_{\nu}(y)dy,\quad J_{\nu,\ell}(x)=D(\nu,\ell)-I_{\nu,\ell}(x).

We have the following asymptotics (see Chapter 2 of
[
34
]
)

(6.67)

I_{\nu,\ell}(z)=z^{\ell-1/2}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\cos(z-\frac{\nu\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{4})+O(z^{\ell-3/2}),\quad z>1;\quad I_{\nu,\ell}(z)=O(1),\quad z\leq 1.

From these asymptotics, we see that for
k,l=1,2

(6.68)

\int_{\lambda}^{g_{N,n}(x)}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(z)]_{k}dz=O(1),\;\;\int_{\lambda}^{g_{N,m}(x)}[\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}(z)]_{l}dz=O(1).

As
h_{N,k}(x),l_{N,l}(x),h_{N,k}^{\prime}(x),l_{N,l}^{\prime}(x)=O(1)
uniformly, and as
g_{N,n}(x)=(N-k)f_{N,n,\lambda}(x)
, we thus see that
II,III=O(N^{-2})
.

We may further rewrite
I
as the sum of two terms

(6.69)

I^{\prime}=-\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\sqrt{g_{N,n}(x)}J_{\alpha-(-1)^{k}1/2}(g_{N,n}(x))I_{\alpha-(-1)^{l}1/2,1/2}(g_{N,m}(x))\frac{h_{N,k}(x)l_{N,l}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)}dx

(6.70)

II^{\prime}=\sum_{k,l=1,2}D(\alpha-(-1)^{l}1/2,1/2)\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}J_{\alpha-(-1)^{k}1/2}(g_{N,n}(x))\frac{h_{N,k}(x)l_{N,l}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)}dx.

We have that
II^{\prime}=O(N^{-2})
by Lemma
6.7
. Finally, employing the asymptotics for
J_{\mu}
and
I_{\mu,\ell}
given by (
6.67
) and (
6.31
) one derives that

(6.71)

I^{\prime}=-\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\cos(g_{N,n}(x)-\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}+\frac{((-1)^{k}-1)\pi}{4})\times

(6.72)

\cos(g_{N,m}(x)-\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}+\frac{((-1)^{l}+1)\pi}{4})\frac{h_{N,k}(x)l_{N,l}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)}dx+O(N^{-2})=

(6.73)

-\sum_{k,l=1,2}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}[\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{k}[\mathcal{I}_{\alpha-1}(g_{N,m}(x))]_{l}\frac{h_{N,k}(x)l_{N,l}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)}dx+O(N^{-2}).

From the error bounds on
R_{N}
and
\bar{R}_{N}
, we see that
h_{N,i}(x)=\phi(x)[T_{N,n,\lambda}(x)]_{1i}+O(N^{-1})
and
l_{N,i}(x)=\varphi(x)[T_{N,m,\lambda}(x)]_{1i}+O(N^{-1})
, uniformly. Applying these, we can further write
I^{\prime}
as

(6.74)

-\frac{1}{\pi(N-m)}\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}[\mathcal{I}_{\alpha}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{k}[\mathcal{I}_{\alpha-1}(g_{N,m}(x))]_{l}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)[T_{N,n,\lambda}(x)]_{1k}[T_{N,m,\lambda}(x)]_{1l}}{f_{N,m,\lambda}^{\prime}(x)}dx+O(N^{-2}).

Recalling that
g_{N,k}(x)=(N-k)f_{N,k,\lambda}(x)=(N-k)(s_{N,k}(\lambda)-s_{N,k}(x))
, we see that by Lemma
6.4

(6.75)

I^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{\pi(N-m)}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}[T_{\infty}(x)e^{-i\pi\sum_{p=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{p}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(N-n)s_{N,n}(x)}\\
e^{-i(N-n)s_{N,n}(x)}\end{bmatrix}]_{1}\times

(6.76)

[T_{\infty}(x)e^{-i\pi\sum_{p=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{p}\sigma}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{i(N-m)s_{N,m}(x)}\\
e^{-i(N-m)s_{N,m}(x)}\end{bmatrix}]_{1}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{|s_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)|}dx+O(N^{-2}).

Employing Lemma
4.4
, we may now expand the integral on the right-hand side of (
6.76
) as

(6.77)

-\frac{i}{\pi N}\sum_{k,l=1,2}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}e^{-(-1)^{k}Nis(x)-(-1)^{l}Nis(x)}\frac{f_{k,l}(x)}{|s^{\prime}(x)|}dx+O(N^{-2}),

where here

(6.78)

f_{k,l}(x)=\phi(x)e^{(-1)^{k}in\mathrm{arccos}(x)}[T_{\infty}(x)]_{1k}e^{(-1)^{k}i\pi\sum_{p=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{p}}\varphi(x)(-1)^{l}e^{(-1)^{l}im\mathrm{arccos}(x)}[T_{\infty}(x)]_{1l}e^{(-1)^{l}i\pi\sum_{p=1}^{j-1}\alpha_{p}}.

We see that by integration by parts (
6.8
) that the terms with
l=k
in (
6.77
) are
O(N^{-2})
. Thus (
6.77
) may further be written as

(6.79)

-\frac{i}{\pi N}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{f_{1,2}(x)}{|s^{\prime}(x)|}+\frac{f_{2,1}(x)}{|s^{\prime}(x)|}\bigg)dx+O(N^{-2})=

(6.80)

-\frac{i}{\pi N}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(x)(e^{-i(n-m)\mathrm{arccos}(x)}-e^{i(n-m)\mathrm{arccos}(x)})\frac{[T_{\infty}(x)]_{11}[T_{\infty}(x)]_{12}}{|s^{\prime}(x)|}dx+O(N^{-2}).

The identities at the end of Lemma
6.2
now show that this coincides with

(6.81)

I^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\pi N}\int_{\lambda}^{\infty}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}\sin((m-n)\arccos(x))dx+O(N^{-2})

This completes the evaluation of the first integral on the right-hand side of (
6.60
). The second integral is similarly given by

(6.82)

\frac{1}{2\pi N}\int_{-\infty}^{\lambda}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}\sin((m-n)\arccos(x))dx+O(N^{-2}).

Together these computations complete the proof of the second claim.
∎

To compute the integrals supported around
\{\pm 1\}
we will need a lemma similar to Lemma
6.4
. We recall that for
x>0
we have that (Chapter 10 of
[
1
]
)

(6.83)

\mathrm{Ai}(-x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}x^{1/4}}(\sin(\frac{2}{3}x^{3/2}+\pi/4)+O(x^{-3/2})),\;\;\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(-x)=-\frac{x^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\pi}}(\cos(\frac{2}{3}x^{3/2}+\pi/4)+O(x^{-3/2})).

Similarly to Lemma
6.4
, we have the following matching formula.

Lemma 6.8
.

We have for
|x|\leq 1
that

(6.84)

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}T_{1}(x)\begin{bmatrix}|f_{1}(x)|^{-1/4}\sin(a+\pi/4)\\
-|f_{1}(x)|^{1/4}\cos(a+\pi/4)\end{bmatrix}=T_{\infty}(x)\begin{bmatrix}e^{ia}\\
e^{-ia}\end{bmatrix},

and that

(6.85)

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}T_{-1}(x)\begin{bmatrix}|f_{-1}(x)|^{-1/4}\sin(a+\pi/4)\\
|f_{-1}(x)|^{1/4}\cos(a+\pi/4)\end{bmatrix}=T_{\infty}(x)e^{-i\pi\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}e^{ia}\\
-e^{-ia}\end{bmatrix}.

Proof.

We observe that

(6.86)

e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}\sin(a+\pi/4)\\
-\cos(a+\pi/4)\end{bmatrix}=e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
-i&-1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sin(a+\pi/4)\\
-\cos(a+\pi/4)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}e^{ia}\\
e^{-ia}\end{bmatrix}.

Applying
T_{\infty}
and recalling the definition of
T_{1}
given in (
4.53
) then achieves the first equality. The proof of the result for
T_{-1}
follows similarly.
∎

We will also need the following computation.

Lemma 6.9
.

We have that

(6.87)

[T_{1}(1)]_{11}=\sqrt{2\pi},\;\;[T_{-1}(-1)]_{11}=\sqrt{2\pi}.

Proof.

We note that
D(x)=\exp(\mathcal{W}(x))(1+O(|x-1|^{1/2}))
(see the proof of Lemma 4.15 in
[
4
]
) and as in addition
f_{1}^{\prime}(1)=2
, we see that for
x>1

(6.88)

T_{1}(x)=\sqrt{\pi}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}A(x)e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}2^{\sigma/4}(x-1)^{\sigma/4}+O((x-1)^{1/4}).

We note that for
x>1
we have that

(6.89)

A(x)=\frac{1}{2^{3/4}(x-1)^{1/4}}\begin{bmatrix}1&i\\
-i&1\end{bmatrix}+\frac{(x-1)^{1/4}}{2^{5/4}}\begin{bmatrix}1&-i\\
i&1\end{bmatrix}+O((x-1)^{3/4}).

From this, one may routinely obtain that

(6.90)

\sqrt{\pi}e^{-i\frac{\pi}{4}}A(x)e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\sigma}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}2^{\sigma/4}=\sqrt{2\pi}(x-1)^{-1/4}\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
-i&0\end{bmatrix}+i\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}(x-1)^{1/4}\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
0&-i\end{bmatrix}+O((x-1)^{3/4}).

Together these are sufficient to establish that
[T_{1}(1)]_{11}=\sqrt{2\pi}
. The case of
-1
is similar.
∎

With these results established, we are now able to compute the relevant integrals in the regions around
\{\pm 1\}
. This result will follow from similar methods to Lemma
6.6
, though we will contend with a variety of complications as the integrand switches from being oscillatory to exponentially decaying. Particularly, this transition will cause a variety of boundary terms to appear, which requires us to carefully analyze each term carefully to get the exact contribution. In addition, the non-smooth nature of many of the functions composing
T_{\pm 1}
will require us to carefully Taylor expand many quantities to show cancellation.

We note that we will only need this level of detail for one integral of Proposition
2.9
, which is recalled as (
6.169
) below, and showing simply that (
6.93
) is simply
O(N^{-1})
is significantly simpler.

Lemma 6.10
.

There is
\delta>0
, such that if
\phi
and
\varphi
are smooth functions supported on
(\pm 1-\delta,\pm 1+\delta)
, then we have for any
n
and
m
that

(6.91)

\int\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=\frac{1}{(2N)^{1/2}}(\pm 1)^{N-n}\phi(\pm 1)+O(N^{-5/6}),

(6.92)

\frac{1}{2}\int\int\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=

(6.93)

\frac{1}{2\pi N}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}\sin((m-n)\mathrm{arccos}(x))dx+O(N^{-7/6}).

Proof.

Fix
\delta
as in Lemma
6.6
. We demonstrate the case of
+1
, the case of
-1
being identical. We first recall that there is
C
such that
|\mathrm{Ai}(x)|,|\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(x)|\leq Ce^{-x}
(see Chapter 10 of
[
1
]
). From the asymptotics of Proposition
4.10
we see that

(6.94)

\int_{1}^{\infty}\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=\frac{1}{\pi^{1/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(N-n)^{1/6}h_{N,1}(x)\mathrm{Ai}((N-n)^{2/3}x)dx+

(6.95)

\frac{1}{\pi^{1/2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}(N-n)^{-1/6}h_{N,2}(x)\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}((N-n)^{2/3}x)dx,

where here
h_{N,i}(x)=(f_{N,n,1}^{-1})^{\prime}(x)[(I+R_{N}(f_{N,n,1}^{-1}(x)))T_{1}(f_{N,n,1}^{-1}(x))]_{1i}\phi(f_{N,n,1}^{-1}(x))
. Noting that
h_{N,2}
is uniformly bounded on
(1,\infty)
, we see that by the exponential bound on
\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}
the second integral on the right-hand side of (
6.95
) is
O(N^{-5/6})
, so we may focus on the first integral on the right-hand side. As
h_{N,1}(x)
is smooth and of compact support, we see that there is
C
such that
|h_{N,1}(x)-h_{N,1}(0)|\leq C|x|
. In particular,

(6.96)

|\int_{0}^{\infty}(h_{N,1}(x)-h_{N,1}(0))\mathrm{Ai}((N-n)^{2/3}x)dx|\leq C\int^{\infty}_{0}|x\mathrm{Ai}((N-n)^{2/3}x)|dx.

Using that
\mathrm{Ai}
is subexponential, we see there are
C>0
such that

(6.97)

\int^{\infty}_{0}|x\mathrm{Ai}((N-n)^{2/3}x)|dx\leq C(N-n)^{-4/3}\int_{0}^{\infty}xe^{-x}dx=O(N^{-4/3}),

so that

(6.98)

\int_{0}^{\infty}h_{N,1}(x)\mathrm{Ai}((N-n)^{2/3}x)dx=h_{N,1}(0)\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ai}((N-n)^{2/3}x)dx+O(N^{-4/3}).

As
f^{\prime}_{1}(1)=2
, we see that
h_{N,1}(0)=2^{-1}\phi(1)[T_{1}(1)]_{11}+O(N^{-1})
, and thus (
6.95
) may be rewritten as

(6.99)

\frac{1}{2\pi^{1/2}}N^{-1/2}\phi(1)[T_{1}(1)]_{11}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ai}(x)dx+O(N^{-5/6}).

Applying Lemma
6.9
and the fact that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ai}(x)dx=\frac{1}{3}
(see 10.4.82 of
[
1
]
) we see that we may further reduce this to

(6.100)

\phi(1)\frac{1}{(2N)^{1/2}}(\frac{1}{3})+O(N^{-5/6}).

To compute the integral in the region
(1-\delta,1)
we observe that we have that

(6.101)

\mathrm{Ai}(-x)=\frac{\sqrt{x}}{3}(J_{1/3}(\frac{2}{3}x^{3/2})+J_{-1/3}(\frac{2}{3}x^{3/2})),

(6.102)

\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(-x)=\frac{x}{3}(J_{2/3}(\frac{2}{3}x^{3/2})-J_{-2/3}(\frac{2}{3}x^{3/2})),

which occur as (10.4.15) and (10.4.17) of
[
1
]
. By applying Lemma
6.7
to the terms in this expression, we obtain that for smooth, compactly-supported
h
, we have that

(6.103)

\int_{0}^{\infty}h(t)\mathrm{Ai}(-N^{2/3}t)dt=\frac{h(0)}{N^{2/3}}\frac{2}{3}+O(N^{-4/3}),\;\;\;\int_{0}^{\infty}h(t)\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(-N^{2/3}t)dt=\frac{h(0)}{3^{2/3}\Gamma(2/3)N^{2/3}}+O(N^{-4/3}),

where the error can similarly be expressed in terms of the derivatives of
h
. Proceeding similarly to the case of
(1,1+\delta)
, and noting that

(6.104)

\int_{-\infty}^{1}\phi(x)p_{N-n}(x)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}dx=\frac{\phi(1)}{(2N)^{1/2}}(\frac{2}{3})+O(N^{-5/6}).

Together these yield (
6.91
).

We now proceed to derive the double integral result. We observe that in view of (
6.104
), (
6.100
) and (
6.2
), we have that

(6.105)

\int_{\mp\infty}^{1}\int_{1}^{\pm\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=\pm\frac{\phi(1)\varphi(1)}{9N}+O(N^{-4/3}),

so that splitting the domain of the double integral into four regions, we see that

(6.106)

\int\int\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=

(6.107)

\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{1}^{\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy+

(6.108)

\int^{1}_{-\infty}\int^{1}_{-\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy+O(N^{-4/3}).

To evaluate these integrals, we will denote
h_{N,i}(x)=[(I+R_{N}(x))T_{1}(x)]_{1i}\phi(x)
and
l_{N,i}(x)=[(I+\bar{R}_{N}(x))T_{1}(x)]_{1i}\varphi(x)
, where
R_{N}
and
\bar{R}_{N}
denote the error terms in Proposition
4.10
for case of
k=n
and
k=m
, respectively. Let us denote

(6.109)

\mathcal{A}_{N,k}(x)=\begin{bmatrix}(N-k)^{1/6}\mathrm{Ai}(x)\\
(N-k)^{-1/6}\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(x)\end{bmatrix},

and
g_{N,k}(x)=N^{2/3}f_{N,k,1}(x)
. With this notation, we may write the integral over the region
(1,\infty)^{2}
as

(6.110)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{1}^{\infty}h_{N,i}(x)l_{N,j}(y)[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{i}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(g_{N,m}(y))]_{j}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy.

We note that for
l,k\in\{1,2\}
, there is
C,c>0
such that for
x\geq 1
,

(6.111)

|[\mathcal{A}_{N,l}(g_{N,m}(x))]_{k}|\leq CN^{-(-1)^{k}/6}e^{-cN^{2/3}x}.

Applying this and noting that
h_{N,i},l_{N,i}=O(1)
, we see that we see that
(i,j)
-th term in (
6.110
) is of order
O(N^{-4/3-(-1)^{i}/6-(-1)^{j}/6})
, so that it suffices to treat the
i=j=1
term. Arguing with Taylor’s theorem as in the single integral case, we see that the integral

(6.112)

\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{1}^{\infty}h_{N,1}(x)(g_{N,1}(y)-\frac{g_{N,1}(1)}{h_{N,1}(1)}h_{N,1}(y))[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{1}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(g_{N,m}(y))]_{1}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy

is of order
O(N^{-4/3})
. Noting that
|g_{N,n}(x)-g_{N,m}(x)|=O(N^{-1/3})
, we see by the above estimate on
\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}
, as well as Taylor’s theorem again, that we see that there is
C,c>0
such that for
x\in(1,1+\delta)

(6.113)

|[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{1}-[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(g_{N,m}(x))]_{1}|\leq CN^{-1/6}e^{-cN^{2/3}x},

so that

(6.114)

\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{1}^{\infty}h_{N,1}(x)h_{N,1}(y)[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{1}([\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(g_{N,n}(y))]_{1}-[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(g_{N,m}(y))]_{1})\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy

is of order
O(N^{-4/3})
. Now noting that by symmetry, we have that

(6.115)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{1}^{\infty}h_{N,1}(x)h_{N,1}(y)[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{1}[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(y))]_{1}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy=0,

we see combining these results that the
i=j=1
term in (
6.110
) is of order
O(N^{-4/3})
, so that in total we have that

(6.116)

\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{1}^{\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=O(N^{-4/3}).

Combining these to show (
6.93
), we see that it suffices to show that

(6.117)

\frac{1}{2}\int^{1}_{-\infty}\int^{1}_{-\infty}\phi(x)\varphi(y)p_{N-n}(x)p_{N-m}(y)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)w_{N}(x)^{1/2}w_{N}(y)^{1/2}dxdy=

(6.118)

\frac{1}{2\pi N}\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\phi(x)\varphi(x)}{(1-x^{2})}\sin((m-n)\mathrm{arccos}(x))dx+O(N^{-7/6}).

As above, we write the integral over
(-\infty,1)^{2}
as

(6.119)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{i,j=1,2}\int_{-\infty}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{1}h_{N,i}(x)l_{N,j}(y)[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{i}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(g_{N,m}(y))]_{j}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy.

Except for the case of
i=j=1
, all of these terms may be dealt with essentially as in the proof of Lemma
6.6
. On the other hand, the
i=j=1
term will require an amount of technical care, as did for the integral over
(1,\infty)^{2}
. For this reason, instead of employing Lemma
6.5
directly, we will need to modify its proof by integrating by parts using
\int_{-y}^{\infty}
rather than
\int_{0}^{y}
. This will be convenient as otherwise, each integral in Lemma
6.5
would still be of leading order for the
i=j=1
term.

To begin, we note that by integration by parts, we may write

(6.120)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{1}l_{N,j}(y)[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(g_{N,m}(y))]_{j}\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dy=\frac{l_{N,j}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)}(\int_{-g_{N,m}(x)}^{\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz)

(6.121)

-\frac{1}{2}\frac{l_{N,j}(1)}{g^{\prime}_{N,m}(1)}(\int_{-g_{N,m}(1)}^{\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{1}(\int_{-g_{N,m}(y)}^{\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz)\frac{d}{dy}\left(\frac{l_{N,j}(y)}{g^{\prime}_{N,m}(y)}\right)\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dy.

Employing this, we see that we may write the
(i,j)
-th term of (
6.119
) as the sum
I_{ij}+II_{ij}+III_{ij}^{\prime}
where

(6.122)

I_{ij}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{1}[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{i}(\int^{\infty}_{-g_{N,m}(x)}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz)\frac{h_{N,i}(x)l_{N,j}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)}dx,

(6.123)

II_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}(\int^{1}_{-\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{i}h_{N,i}(x)dx)\frac{l_{N,j}(1)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(1)}\int_{-g_{N,m}(1)}^{\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz,

(6.124)

III^{\prime}_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{1}\int_{-\infty}^{1}[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(g_{N,n}(x))]_{i}(\int^{\infty}_{-g_{N,m}(y)}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz)h_{N,i}(x)\frac{d}{dy}(\frac{l_{N,j}(y)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(y)})\mathrm{Sign}(x-y)dxdy.

By a similar integration by parts we may write
III^{\prime}_{ij}
itself as a sum
III_{ij}+IV_{ij}+V_{ij}
with

(6.125)

III_{ij}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{1}(\int^{\infty}_{-g_{N,n}(x)}[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(-z)]_{i}dz)(\int^{\infty}_{-g_{N,m}(x)}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz)\frac{h_{N,i}(x)}{g_{N,n}^{\prime}(x)}\frac{d}{dx}(\frac{l_{N,j}(x)}{g_{N,m}^{\prime}(x)})dx,

(6.126)

IV_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{h_{N,i}(1)}{g_{N,n}^{\prime}(1)}\int_{-g_{N,n}(1)}^{\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{N,n}(-z)]_{i}dz\int_{-\infty}^{1}\frac{d}{dy}(\frac{l_{N,j}(y)}{g^{\prime}_{N,m}(y)})(\int_{-g_{N,m}(y)}^{\infty}[\mathcal{A}_{N,m}(-z)]_{j}dz)dy.

(6.127)
